"""(3-hop capacity) chained h(f(g(X))) over ONE installed operand — depth-as-fuel.

Pre-reg: mementum/knowledge/explore/three-hop-capacity-prereg.md (APPROVED s282,
geography chain FROZEN). Successor to the 2-hop wrapper/operand_multihop.py (s279).

Chain (geography): landmark --g--> city --f--> country --h--> continent.
  X = a nonce carrying a LANDMARK's content d_E (built like the 2-hop d_E, last-token
  capture of a multi-token phrase). Two UNSTATED bridges (city, country) never appear in
  the readout prompt. Final readout = closed 3-way continent {Europe, Asia, Africa}.

Framing (s280 depth-budget): this is a CAPACITY experiment, not a capability rung. A
full-chain failure counts as DEPTH-limited ONLY IF the pieces work on the same model
(Gate-2 sub-chain controls). Pre-registered predictions: Qwen3-4B -> FAIL-BY-CAPACITY
(controls pass, full chain fails); Qwen3-32B -> PASS (full + mediation). The double
dissociation across scale, pieces held constant, is the strongest C8 evidence available.

Gates (frozen in the pre-reg):
  Gate 1  FULL CHAIN    : install landmark, "The {nonce} ... continent of __"
                          -> continent; nulls = random install, baseline, content-spec.
  Gate 2  SUB-CHAINS    : (the capacity discriminator)
            S1 links     : landmark->city, city->country, country->continent at ceiling.
            2-hop g.f    : install landmark -> its COUNTRY.
            2-hop f.h    : install CITY -> its CONTINENT (the s279-style 2-hop).
  Gate 3  MEDIATION     : (only where Gate-1 passes)
            3a depth-order : logit-lens peaks ordered city < country < continent.
            3b country-swap: late country-axis swap flips continent (random does not).
            3c city-swap   : mid city-axis swap flips country+continent (random not).

`λ measure`: operand = VALUE (d_E, centroids); g,f,h = ROUTING; readout = logits;
bridges localized by DEPTH (3a) + zone-steer (3b/3c), never single-head (0/128 heads).
`λ yardstick`: nulls beside every number; real-word ceiling gates each cell; predict
a-priori, gate on nulls, no forced fit. A RUNG (capacity mapping), hook-not-weight.
Architecture-robust via resolve_parts (dense Qwen3 4B/32B; hybrid 27B follow-on).

License: MIT (`λ provenance`; SuperBake method-reference only).
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import json
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import torch
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer

# ── geography ladder: landmark -> (city, country, continent). Balanced 3-way. ──────
# Multi-token landmarks/cities handled by last-token capture (d_E) and first-token
# grading (closed candidate sets). Obscure cells are pruned by the real-word ceiling.
LANDMARKS: dict[str, tuple[str, str, str]] = {
    # Europe
    "Colosseum":      ("Rome",         "Italy",        "Europe"),
    "Louvre":         ("Paris",        "France",       "Europe"),
    "Parthenon":      ("Athens",       "Greece",       "Europe"),
    "Kremlin":        ("Moscow",       "Russia",       "Europe"),
    "Sagrada Familia":("Barcelona",    "Spain",        "Europe"),
    "Brandenburg Gate":("Berlin",      "Germany",      "Europe"),
    # Asia
    "Taj Mahal":      ("Agra",         "India",        "Asia"),
    "Kaaba":          ("Mecca",        "Saudi Arabia", "Asia"),
    "Petronas Towers":("Kuala Lumpur", "Malaysia",     "Asia"),
    "Angkor Wat":     ("Siem Reap",    "Cambodia",     "Asia"),
    "Tiananmen":      ("Beijing",      "China",        "Asia"),
    "Burj Khalifa":   ("Dubai",        "UAE",          "Asia"),
    # Africa
    "Pyramids":       ("Giza",         "Egypt",        "Africa"),
    "Sphinx":         ("Giza",         "Egypt",        "Africa"),
    "Karnak":         ("Luxor",        "Egypt",        "Africa"),
    "Table Mountain": ("Cape Town",    "South Africa", "Africa"),
    "Medina":         ("Marrakech",    "Morocco",      "Africa"),
    "Victoria Falls": ("Livingstone",  "Zambia",       "Africa"),
}

CONTINENTS = ["Europe", "Asia", "Africa"]
LM_LIST = list(LANDMARKS)
CITY_OF = {lm: v[0] for lm, v in LANDMARKS.items()}
COUNTRY_OF = {lm: v[1] for lm, v in LANDMARKS.items()}
CONT_OF = {lm: v[2] for lm, v in LANDMARKS.items()}
CITIES = sorted(set(CITY_OF.values()))
COUNTRIES = sorted(set(COUNTRY_OF.values()))
# city -> country/continent (deterministic; Giza collision is consistent)
CITY_COUNTRY = {v[0]: v[1] for v in LANDMARKS.values()}
CITY_CONT = {v[0]: v[2] for v in LANDMARKS.values()}
COUNTRY_CONT = {v[1]: v[2] for v in LANDMARKS.values()}
NONCE = "zorp"

# held-out exemplars (disjoint from the test landmarks/cities/countries)
CONT_PREFIX = (
    "The Alhambra is located on the continent of Europe.\n"
    "The Great Wall is located on the continent of Asia.\n"
    "The Serengeti is located on the continent of Africa.\n"
)
COUNTRY_PREFIX = (
    "The Alhambra is located in the country of Spain.\n"
    "The Great Wall is located in the country of China.\n"
    "The Serengeti is located in the country of Tanzania.\n"
)
CITY_PREFIX = (
    "The Alhambra is located in the city of Granada.\n"
    "The Great Wall is located in the city of Beijing.\n"
    "The Colosseum is located in the city of Rome.\n"
)
CITY2COUNTRY_PREFIX = (
    "The city of Lisbon is located in the country of Portugal.\n"
    "The city of Nairobi is located in the country of Kenya.\n"
    "The city of Osaka is located in the country of Japan.\n"
)
COUNTRY2CONT_PREFIX = (
    "The country of Portugal is located on the continent of Europe.\n"
    "The country of Japan is located on the continent of Asia.\n"
    "The country of Kenya is located on the continent of Africa.\n"
)

CONT_QUERY = "The {x} is located on the continent of"
COUNTRY_QUERY = "The {x} is located in the country of"
CITY_QUERY = "The {x} is located in the city of"
CITY2COUNTRY_QUERY = "The city of {x} is located in the country of"
COUNTRY2CONT_QUERY = "The country of {x} is located on the continent of"

# d_E build: cross-task declaratives (entity at END, before period -> capture -2)
FRAMES = [
    "The travelers admired {x}.",
    "A postcard showed {x}.",
    "The documentary featured {x}.",
    "The guidebook described {x}.",
    "Tourists photographed {x}.",
    "The lecture mentioned {x}.",
    "A painting depicted {x}.",
    "The article discussed {x}.",
]


def first_tid(tok, w):
    return tok(" " + w, add_special_tokens=False).input_ids[0]


def cap_hook(store, li):
    def hook(_m, _i, out):
        h = out[0] if isinstance(out, tuple) else out
        store[li] = h.detach().float().cpu().numpy()
    return hook


def add_hook_at(vec_t, pos):
    def hook(_m, _i, out):
        h = out[0] if isinstance(out, tuple) else out
        if 0 <= pos < h.shape[1]:
            h[0, pos, :] = h[0, pos, :] + vec_t.to(h.dtype)
        return out
    return hook


def resolve_parts(model):
    """(decoder-layers, final-norm, lm_head) across architectures (dense + hybrid)."""
    inner = model.model
    lm = inner if hasattr(inner, "layers") else inner.language_model
    return lm.layers, lm.norm, model.lm_head


def main() -> None:
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("--model-id", default="Qwen/Qwen3-4B")
    ap.add_argument("--ref-layer", type=int, default=9)          # install layer
    ap.add_argument("--scale", type=float, default=2.0)
    ap.add_argument("--swap-layers", type=int, nargs="+", default=[11, 15, 20])
    ap.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16", choices=["float32", "bfloat16"])
    ap.add_argument("--device", default="mps")
    ap.add_argument("--mode", default="full", choices=["ceiling", "full"])
    ap.add_argument("--out", default="results/ffn-bake/operand-multihop3-qwen3-4b")
    args = ap.parse_args()

    L = args.ref_layer
    dev = (args.device if (args.device != "mps" or torch.backends.mps.is_available())
           else "cpu")
    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(args.model_id)
    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
        args.model_id, dtype=getattr(torch, args.dtype)).to(dev).eval()
    dec, norm_f, unembed = resolve_parts(model)
    S = args.scale

    cont_ids = {c: first_tid(tok, c) for c in CONTINENTS}
    country_ids = {c: first_tid(tok, c) for c in COUNTRIES}
    city_ids = {c: first_tid(tok, c) for c in CITIES}
    nonce_last = tok(" " + NONCE, add_special_tokens=False).input_ids[-1]
    print(f"[mh3] {args.model_id} L={L} scale={S} dev={dev} "
          f"n={NONCE!r} mode={args.mode}")

    def find_slot(ids_list):
        idx = [i for i, t in enumerate(ids_list) if t == nonce_last]
        return idx[-1] if idx else len(ids_list) - 1

    def pred_over(prefix, query, word, label_ids, adds=None):
        """argmax over a CLOSED candidate set (first-token logits) at nonce slot."""
        prompt = prefix + query.format(x=word)
        ids = tok(prompt, return_tensors="pt").to(dev)
        slot = find_slot(ids.input_ids[0].tolist())
        handles = []
        for (li, vec) in (adds or []):
            vt = torch.tensor(vec, dtype=torch.float32, device=dev)
            handles.append(dec[li].register_forward_hook(add_hook_at(vt, slot)))
        with torch.no_grad():
            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()
        for hd in handles:
            hd.remove()
        return max(label_ids, key=lambda k: lo[label_ids[k]])

    def cont_pred(word, adds=None):
        return pred_over(CONT_PREFIX, CONT_QUERY, word, cont_ids, adds)

    def country_pred(word, adds=None):
        return pred_over(COUNTRY_PREFIX, COUNTRY_QUERY, word, country_ids, adds)

    def city_pred(word, adds=None):
        return pred_over(CITY_PREFIX, CITY_QUERY, word, city_ids, adds)

    # ── S1 links (real word, no install) = the knowledge ceiling ──────────────────
    link_lm_city = {lm: int(city_pred(lm) == CITY_OF[lm]) for lm in LM_LIST}
    link_city_country = {
        c: int(pred_over(CITY2COUNTRY_PREFIX, CITY2COUNTRY_QUERY, c, country_ids)
               == CITY_COUNTRY[c]) for c in CITIES}
    link_country_cont = {
        c: int(pred_over(COUNTRY2CONT_PREFIX, COUNTRY2CONT_QUERY, c, cont_ids)
               == COUNTRY_CONT[c]) for c in COUNTRIES}

    def links_ok(lm):
        return (link_lm_city[lm]
                and link_city_country[CITY_OF[lm]]
                and link_country_cont[COUNTRY_OF[lm]])

    valid = [lm for lm in LM_LIST if links_ok(lm)]
    s1_lm_city = round(float(np.mean(list(link_lm_city.values()))), 3)
    s1_city_country = round(float(np.mean(list(link_city_country.values()))), 3)
    s1_country_cont = round(float(np.mean(list(link_country_cont.values()))), 3)
    by_cont = {c: sum(CONT_OF[lm] == c for lm in valid) for c in CONTINENTS}
    print(f"[mh3] S1 links: lm->city={s1_lm_city} city->country={s1_city_country} "
          f"country->cont={s1_country_cont}")
    print(f"[mh3] valid landmarks: {len(valid)}/{len(LM_LIST)} "
          f"per-continent={by_cont}  {valid}")

    if args.mode == "ceiling":
        out = Path(args.out)
        out.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        (out / "ceiling.json").write_text(json.dumps(
            {"s1_lm_city": s1_lm_city, "s1_city_country": s1_city_country,
             "s1_country_cont": s1_country_cont, "valid": valid,
             "per_continent": by_cont, "link_lm_city": link_lm_city,
             "link_city_country": link_city_country,
             "link_country_cont": link_country_cont}, indent=2))
        print(f"[mh3] ceiling mode: wrote {out}/ceiling.json")
        return

    # ── content directions: d_landmark, d_city, d_country (per-pool mean removed) ──
    def build_dirs(items, cap_L):
        per = {e: [] for e in items}
        for fr in FRAMES:
            for e in items:
                store: dict[int, np.ndarray] = {}
                h = dec[cap_L].register_forward_hook(cap_hook(store, cap_L))
                ids = tok(fr.format(x=e), return_tensors="pt").to(dev)
                with torch.no_grad():
                    model(**ids)
                h.remove()
                per[e].append(store[cap_L][0, -2, :])   # entity last subtoken
        em = {e: np.mean(per[e], axis=0) for e in items}
        gm = np.mean([em[e] for e in items], axis=0)
        return {e: em[e] - gm for e in items}, gm.shape[0]

    d_lm, dim = build_dirs(LM_LIST, L)
    d_city, _ = build_dirs(CITIES, L)
    d_country, _ = build_dirs(COUNTRIES, L)

    def rand_vec(norm):
        v = rng.standard_normal(dim)
        return v / (np.linalg.norm(v) + 1e-9) * norm

    # ══ GATE 1 — FULL CHAIN (install landmark -> continent) ══════════════════════
    def full_acc(use_rand=False):
        hits, cells = 0, {}
        for lm in valid:
            dv = (rand_vec(np.linalg.norm(d_lm[lm]) * S) if use_rand else d_lm[lm] * S)
            pred = cont_pred(NONCE, adds=[(L, dv)])
            ok = int(pred == CONT_OF[lm])
            cells[lm] = {"pred": pred, "truth": CONT_OF[lm], "ok": ok}
            hits += ok
        return (hits / len(valid) if valid else 0.0), cells

    g1_acc, g1_cells = full_acc()
    g1_rand, _ = full_acc(use_rand=True)
    g1_base = (sum(int(cont_pred(NONCE) == CONT_OF[lm]) for lm in valid) / len(valid)
               if valid else 0.0)
    print(f"\n[GATE1] full-chain install acc={g1_acc:.3f} (rand {g1_rand:.3f}, "
          f"baseline {g1_base:.3f}, n={len(valid)})")

    # content-specificity: install two landmarks of DIFFERENT continents -> both follow
    spec = []
    for lm in valid:
        for lp in valid:
            if CONT_OF[lm] == CONT_OF[lp]:
                continue
            pe = cont_pred(NONCE, adds=[(L, d_lm[lm] * S)])
            pp = cont_pred(NONCE, adds=[(L, d_lm[lp] * S)])
            spec.append(int(pe == CONT_OF[lm] and pp == CONT_OF[lp]))
    g1_spec = round(float(np.mean(spec)), 3) if spec else None
    print(f"[GATE1] content-specificity={g1_spec} (n={len(spec)})")

    # ══ GATE 2 — 2-HOP SUB-CHAINS (the capacity discriminator) ════════════════════
    # g.f : install landmark -> its COUNTRY
    def gof_acc(use_rand=False):
        hits, cells = 0, {}
        for lm in valid:
            dv = (rand_vec(np.linalg.norm(d_lm[lm]) * S) if use_rand else d_lm[lm] * S)
            pred = country_pred(NONCE, adds=[(L, dv)])
            ok = int(pred == COUNTRY_OF[lm])
            cells[lm] = {"pred": pred, "truth": COUNTRY_OF[lm], "ok": ok}
            hits += ok
        return (hits / len(valid) if valid else 0.0), cells

    gof, gof_cells = gof_acc()
    gof_rand, _ = gof_acc(use_rand=True)
    gof_base = (sum(int(country_pred(NONCE) == COUNTRY_OF[lm]) for lm in valid)
                / len(valid) if valid else 0.0)
    gof_pass = bool(gof > 0.66 and gof > gof_rand + 0.20 and gof > gof_base + 0.20)
    print(f"[GATE2] 2-hop g.f (landmark->country) acc={gof:.3f} "
          f"(rand {gof_rand:.3f}, base {gof_base:.3f}) pass={gof_pass}")

    # f.h : install CITY -> its CONTINENT (only cities whose city->cont link holds)
    valid_cities = [c for c in CITIES
                    if link_city_country[c] and link_country_cont[CITY_COUNTRY[c]]]

    def fh_acc(use_rand=False):
        hits, cells = 0, {}
        for c in valid_cities:
            dv = (rand_vec(np.linalg.norm(d_city[c]) * S)
                  if use_rand else d_city[c] * S)
            pred = cont_pred(NONCE, adds=[(L, dv)])
            ok = int(pred == CITY_CONT[c])
            cells[c] = {"pred": pred, "truth": CITY_CONT[c], "ok": ok}
            hits += ok
        return (hits / len(valid_cities) if valid_cities else 0.0), cells

    fh, fh_cells = fh_acc()
    fh_rand, _ = fh_acc(use_rand=True)
    fh_base = (sum(int(cont_pred(NONCE) == CITY_CONT[c]) for c in valid_cities)
               / len(valid_cities) if valid_cities else 0.0)
    fh_pass = bool(fh > 0.66 and fh > fh_rand + 0.20 and fh > fh_base + 0.20)
    print(f"[GATE2] 2-hop f.h (city->continent) acc={fh:.3f} "
          f"(rand {fh_rand:.3f}, base {fh_base:.3f}, n={len(valid_cities)}) "
          f"pass={fh_pass}")

    s1_pass = bool(s1_lm_city >= 0.8 and s1_city_country >= 0.8
                   and s1_country_cont >= 0.8)
    gate2_controls = bool(s1_pass and gof_pass and fh_pass)
    print(f"[GATE2] S1-links>=0.8={s1_pass} | controls_pass={gate2_controls}")

    # ══ GATE 3 — MEDIATION (only meaningful where Gate-1 passes) ══════════════════
    # 3a DEPTH ORDER: logit-lens peaks city < country < continent (installed landmark)
    def lens_order(lm):
        prompt = CONT_PREFIX + CONT_QUERY.format(x=NONCE)
        ids = tok(prompt, return_tensors="pt").to(dev)
        slot = find_slot(ids.input_ids[0].tolist())
        vt = torch.tensor(d_lm[lm] * S, dtype=torch.float32, device=dev)
        hd = dec[L].register_forward_hook(add_hook_at(vt, slot))
        with torch.no_grad():
            out = model(**ids, output_hidden_states=True)
        hd.remove()
        city, country, cont = CITY_OF[lm], COUNTRY_OF[lm], CONT_OF[lm]
        cty_i, cnt_i, con_i = city_ids[city], country_ids[country], cont_ids[cont]
        oth_city = [city_ids[c] for c in CITIES if c != city]
        oth_ctry = [country_ids[c] for c in COUNTRIES if c != country]
        oth_cont = [cont_ids[c] for c in CONTINENTS if c != cont]
        cm, ctm, com = [], [], []
        for h in out.hidden_states:
            last = h[0, -1, :]
            with torch.no_grad():
                lg = unembed(norm_f(last.unsqueeze(0))).float().cpu().numpy()[0]
            cm.append(lg[cty_i] - max(lg[j] for j in oth_city))
            ctm.append(lg[cnt_i] - max(lg[j] for j in oth_ctry))
            com.append(lg[con_i] - max(lg[j] for j in oth_cont))
        return int(np.argmax(cm)), int(np.argmax(ctm)), int(np.argmax(com))

    city_pk, ctry_pk, cont_pk = [], [], []
    for lm in valid:
        a, b, c = lens_order(lm)
        city_pk.append(a)
        ctry_pk.append(b)
        cont_pk.append(c)
    med_city = float(np.median(city_pk)) if city_pk else 0.0
    med_ctry = float(np.median(ctry_pk)) if ctry_pk else 0.0
    med_cont = float(np.median(cont_pk)) if cont_pk else 0.0
    order_ok = bool(med_city < med_ctry < med_cont)
    # shuffled-label null: random assignment of the three peak-lists
    shuf = [med_city, med_ctry, med_cont]
    rng.shuffle(shuf)
    shuf_ok = bool(shuf[0] < shuf[1] < shuf[2])
    g3a = bool(order_ok and not shuf_ok)
    print(f"\n[GATE3a] median peaks city={med_city} country={med_ctry} "
          f"continent={med_cont} order_ok={order_ok} (shuf_ok={shuf_ok}) pass={g3a}")

    # 3b LATE COUNTRY-SWAP: install landmark; add (d_country[c'] - d_country[c]) at a
    #    late layer -> continent flips to continent(c'). random matched-norm must not.
    def swap_bridge(kind, layers):
        """kind='country' (expect continent flip) or 'city' (country+continent flip)."""
        results = {}
        for lb in layers:
            flips, rflips, n = [], [], 0
            for lm in valid:
                if kind == "country":
                    src, dbank, keyfn = COUNTRY_OF[lm], d_country, COUNTRY_CONT
                    others = [c for c in COUNTRIES if COUNTRY_CONT[c] != CONT_OF[lm]]
                    tgt_of = keyfn
                else:
                    src, dbank, keyfn = CITY_OF[lm], d_city, CITY_CONT
                    others = [c for c in CITIES if CITY_CONT[c] != CONT_OF[lm]]
                    tgt_of = keyfn
                for tgt in others:
                    swap = (dbank[tgt] - dbank[src]) * S
                    pred = cont_pred(NONCE, adds=[(L, d_lm[lm] * S), (lb, swap)])
                    flips.append(int(pred == tgt_of[tgt]))
                    rnd = rand_vec(np.linalg.norm(swap))
                    rpred = cont_pred(NONCE, adds=[(L, d_lm[lm] * S), (lb, rnd)])
                    rflips.append(int(rpred == tgt_of[tgt]))
                    n += 1
            results[str(lb)] = {"flip_to_swapped": round(float(np.mean(flips)), 3),
                                "random_flip": round(float(np.mean(rflips)), 3), "n": n}
            r = results[str(lb)]
            print(f"[GATE3-{kind}] L_b={lb}: flip={r['flip_to_swapped']} "
                  f"(random {r['random_flip']}, n={n})")
        return results

    print("[GATE3b] country-axis swap (expect continent flip):")
    swap_country = swap_bridge("country", args.swap_layers)
    print("[GATE3c] city-axis swap (expect continent flip):")
    swap_city = swap_bridge("city", args.swap_layers)
    best_country = max(swap_country.values(), key=lambda r: r["flip_to_swapped"])
    best_city = max(swap_city.values(), key=lambda r: r["flip_to_swapped"])
    g3b = bool(best_country["flip_to_swapped"] >= 0.5
               and best_country["random_flip"] < 0.34)
    g3c = bool(best_city["flip_to_swapped"] >= 0.5 and best_city["random_flip"] < 0.34)
    print(f"[GATE3] 3b(country)={g3b} 3c(city)={g3c}")

    # ══ VERDICT (pre-registered, frozen) ═════════════════════════════════════════
    gate1 = bool(g1_acc > 0.66 and g1_acc > g1_rand + 0.20
                 and g1_acc > g1_base + 0.20 and (g1_spec or 0) > 0.5)
    gate3 = bool(g3a and (g3b or g3c)) if gate1 else False
    if gate2_controls and not gate1:
        capacity = "FAIL_BY_CAPACITY"       # pieces work, full fails -> depth-limited
    elif gate1 and gate3:
        capacity = "PASS"                    # full chain + mediation
    elif gate1 and not gate3:
        capacity = "PASS_NO_MEDIATION"
    elif not gate2_controls:
        capacity = "VOID_CONTENT"            # a piece failed -> not a depth verdict
    else:
        capacity = "AMBIGUOUS"
    print(f"\n[VERDICT] Gate1(full)={gate1} | Gate2(controls)={gate2_controls} "
          f"| Gate3(mediation)={gate3}")
    print(f"[VERDICT] CAPACITY PATTERN = {capacity}")

    out = Path(args.out)
    out.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    res = {
        "model": args.model_id, "device": dev, "ref_layer": L, "scale": S,
        "nonce": NONCE, "landmarks": {lm: list(v) for lm, v in LANDMARKS.items()},
        "s1_links": {"lm_city": s1_lm_city, "city_country": s1_city_country,
                     "country_cont": s1_country_cont, "pass": s1_pass,
                     "link_lm_city": link_lm_city,
                     "link_city_country": link_city_country,
                     "link_country_cont": link_country_cont},
        "valid": valid, "per_continent": by_cont,
        "gate1_full": {"install_acc": round(g1_acc, 3), "random": round(g1_rand, 3),
                       "baseline": round(g1_base, 3), "content_specificity": g1_spec,
                       "n": len(valid), "cells": g1_cells, "pass": gate1},
        "gate2_controls": {
            "gof_landmark_country": {"acc": round(gof, 3), "random": round(gof_rand, 3),
                                     "baseline": round(gof_base, 3), "pass": gof_pass,
                                     "cells": gof_cells},
            "fh_city_continent": {"acc": round(fh, 3), "random": round(fh_rand, 3),
                                  "baseline": round(fh_base, 3), "n": len(valid_cities),
                                  "pass": fh_pass, "cells": fh_cells},
            "s1_pass": s1_pass, "controls_pass": gate2_controls},
        "gate3_mediation": {
            "depth_order": {"median_city_peak": med_city,
                            "median_country_peak": med_ctry,
                            "median_continent_peak": med_cont, "order_ok": order_ok,
                            "shuffled_ok": shuf_ok, "pass": g3a,
                            "city_peaks": city_pk, "country_peaks": ctry_pk,
                            "continent_peaks": cont_pk},
            "country_swap": {"by_layer": swap_country, "best": best_country,
                             "pass": g3b},
            "city_swap": {"by_layer": swap_city, "best": best_city, "pass": g3c},
            "gate3_pass": gate3},
        "verdict": {"gate1": gate1, "gate2_controls": gate2_controls, "gate3": gate3,
                    "capacity_pattern": capacity}}
    (out / "operand_multihop3.json").write_text(json.dumps(res, indent=2))
    print(f"[mh3] wrote {out}/operand_multihop3.json")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

### Thinking

I'm laying out the core framework for P-HOLO-CAP: treating the medium as a holographic storage system where multiple landmark operand directions are superposed together, with retrieval signal-to-noise ratio degrading gracefully according to a power law rather than hitting a hard capacity wall. The key insight is that this follows the holographic reduced representation superposition capacity law, giving us SNR(k) ∝ √(D/k) as we add more operands. Now I'm working through how to actually manipulate and compose these superposed operand directions.

For readout, the challenge is that with k superposed operands, a query like "The zorp is located on the continent of __" produces a mixed output across all continents due to symmetry. I need a retrieval cue to isolate one component. The cleanest approach is using multiple nonces—each operand gets installed at its own nonce slot in the prompt, and querying a specific nonce acts as the retrieval cue. This maps cleanly onto the attention machinery as crosstalk between slots, and when k=1 it recovers the single-operand behavior exactly. But I'm realizing this might not be the same as the HRR claim, which is about multiple items superposed in a single vector rather than distributed across position slots.

The real test is simpler: superpose k landmark directions in one slot with no binding keys, then measure how each component's margin degrades as k−1 distractor operands from other continents are added. This directly measures whether the network can separate components under interference. a modern-Hopfield associative memory where interference between patterns scales with the number of stored items, and the signal-to-noise ratio depends on model width and capacity. For the concrete design, I'm setting up a prompt structure where nonces get bound to landmarks in a preamble — each nonce appears as a token position in the context, and I'm installing the corresponding semantic vectors at those slots so the model can later retrieve them when queried.

The key insight is that for the query nonce, I install its vector at the query's own slot position (using the last token occurrence), while distractor nonces appear earlier in the preamble with their vectors installed at their respective preamble positions. This preserves the single-slot behavior while keeping all nonces visible for symmetry. I need to generate k distinct nonce tokens — ideally single-token or short multi-token words like "zorp", "flim", "drax" — where the slot-finding mechanism uses the last tokenized occurrence of each nonce.

For the experimental grid, I'm testing k values of {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16}, capped by the minimum of available landmarks and distinct nonces. At 4B parameters I have 17 valid landmarks, at 32B I have 18. For each k value, I sample k landmarks randomly (no continent stratification), shuffle them across the k nonces, and query all k nonces per draw to fully utilize each sample — this means k forward passes per draw, with a total cost around 52 forward passes per draw-set, which should be manageable given the marginal cost of forward passes on short prompts.

With 30 draws across the grid, I'm looking at roughly 1560 forward passes total, or about 30 minutes at 0.35 seconds per forward pass. I'll also run null controls using random vectors and bootstrap noise floors to establish baselines.

For SNR, I'm defining it per component as the margin between the true continent's logit and the highest competing logit, then averaging across components and draws. This keeps the signal definition consistent with the original framework.

On predictions, the key hypothesis is that margin declines smoothly and monotonically with k, following the HRR scaling law where m(k) ∝ k^(−1/2). The test itself is secondary—I'll fit a power-law exponent and compare it against a null distribution of random monotone-decreasing curves over the same range to see if the data actually matches the HRR form.

For the slot/addressed distinction, the primary gate is whether m(k) shows a graceful decline or a sharp cliff, using the same cliff-detection statistic from the fragmentation analysis. But here's the tension: at realistic parameter values like D=5120 and k=16, the retrieval signal-to-noise ratio stays so high (~18) that margin may not decline noticeably at all, which would undermine the distinguishing power between HRR and the null hypothesis.

The real issue is that actual transformers show much worse interference than ideal HRR predicts—attention mixing and content-specificity effects cause measurable degradation. So empirically the margin will decline as distractors accumulate, but the key question becomes how it declines. I'm now defining what "superposition-graceful" actually means: the gate passes, decline is material, there's no sharp cliff, and crucially, the decline pattern is independent of which specific distractor set appears. I need an analog to location-independence here—maybe looking at variance across different distractor draws versus component resampling noise to test composition-independence.

The main discriminator should be whether there's a cliff at some capacity threshold versus smooth degradation, with the √k form as secondary evidence and an identity-independence index to corroborate. I'm also checking the width dimension—comparing 4B and 32B models at matched capacity levels to see if relative decline curves are shallower at larger model widths.

For the nulls, I need to establish a bootstrap noise floor to assess materiality, and then run a control where I mix the target with random distractor vectors to determine whether content-specific interference matters more than just raw energy effects.

I'm also planning planted calibrations in the validation set: a linear superposition model that should show graceful k^(-1/2) scaling with no cliff, a slot-based store with fixed capacity that produces a sharp cliff at the slot limit, and a smooth null for comparison. I'm setting up three experimental arms to isolate the capacity signal: a content arm where distractors are installed at their preamble slots, a prompt-matched baseline where the preamble structure stays the same but distractors aren't installed (just bare nonces), and a random-vector arm where distractor slots get matched-norm random vectors instead. The difference between content and baseline performance reveals the actual capacity effect independent of prompt-shape artifacts.

For the primary analysis, I'm tracking the content curve as the main signal with the bare curve as reference, looking for a cliff in performance at G1/G2 and measuring materiality as either the total decline from k=1 or the gap at maximum k. routing = "routing"        # crisp/discrete: attention patterns, head selection
    value = "value"            # continuous/graded: residual content, subspaces
    contrast = "contrast"      # dark-field / difference channels (Q/M)

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class NullDraws:
    """Draws from a declared null + provenance (recorded at construction)."""
    name: str
    draws: np.ndarray
    provenance: dict = field(default_factory=dict)

    def __post_init__(self):
        object.__setattr__(self, "draws", np.asarray(self.draws, dtype=float))
        if self.draws.size == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"null '{self.name}' produced zero draws")


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Gated:
    """The only object that carries a p-value. warnings NEVER alter data."""
    name: str
    value: float
    null_name: str
    null_mean: float
    null_std: float
    n_draws: int
    predict: str
    alpha: float
    p: float
    sign_ok: bool
    verdict: bool
    warnings: tuple[str, ...] = ()


def gate(value: float, null: NullDraws, predict: str, alpha: float = 0.05,
         name: str = "", claim_register: Register | None = None,
         probe_register: Register | None = None) -> Gated:
    """Compare an observed statistic against a declared null, directionally.

    predict: 'greater' (value predicted above null) or 'less'. Mandatory —
    there is no two-sided option (a prediction has a sign; λ yardstick).
    p is the add-one permutation p in the PREDICTED direction.
    verdict = (p < alpha) AND sign_ok. Wrong-sign extremity is a failure,
    reported verbatim, never flipped."""
    if not isinstance(null, NullDraws):
        raise TypeError("gate() requires a declared NullDraws (no null, no p)")
    if predict not in ("greater", "less"):
        raise ValueError("predict must be 'greater' or 'less' (declared a priori)")
    draws = null.draws
    v = float(value)
    if predict == "greater":
        p = float((1 + np.sum(draws >= v)) / (1 + draws.size))
        sign_ok = v > float(draws.mean())
    else:
        p = float((1 + np.sum(draws <= v)) / (1 + draws.size))
        sign_ok = v < float(draws.mean())
    warnings: list[str] = []
    if claim_register is not None and probe_register is not None \
            and claim_register is not probe_register:
        w = (f"register mismatch: claim={claim_register.value} "
             f"probe={probe_register.value} (s206 scar — verify the probe "
             f"measures the claimed quantity)")
        warnings.append(w)
        print(f"[dsp.gate] WARNING {name}: {w}", file=sys.stderr)
    return Gated(
        name=name, value=v, null_name=null.name,
        null_mean=float(draws.mean()), null_std=float(draws.std()),
        n_draws=int(draws.size), predict=predict, alpha=float(alpha),
        p=p, sign_ok=sign_ok, verdict=bool(p < alpha and sign_ok),
        warnings=tuple(warnings),
    )


# ── constructors ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
def shuffled_label(stat, y: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
                   n_iter: int = 200) -> NullDraws:
    """Full shuffled-label pipeline null: stat(permuted labels), n_iter times.

    stat: callable(label_array) -> float. The stat must RERUN the whole
    downstream pipeline on the shuffled labels (the QK lesson: shuffle ->
    centroids -> subspace -> same mapping -> same statistic), not just
    re-score cached intermediates. NaN draws are dropped (recorded)."""
    draws = []
    for _ in range(n_iter):
        v = float(stat(rng.permutation(y)))
        if not np.isnan(v):
            draws.append(v)
    return NullDraws("shuffled_label", np.array(draws),
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "n_kept": len(draws)})


def matched_random(stat, dim: int, norm: float, rng: np.random.Generator,
                   n_iter: int = 200) -> NullDraws:
    """Matched-norm random-direction null: stat(random unit vector * norm).

    The exact 3b/P-ATT-MED null family: same norm, isotropic direction."""
    draws = []
    for _ in range(n_iter):
        v = rng.standard_normal(dim)
        v *= norm / (np.linalg.norm(v) + 1e-12)
        draws.append(float(stat(v)))
    return NullDraws("matched_random", np.array(draws),
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "dim": dim, "norm": float(norm)})


def paired_permutation(a: np.ndarray, b: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
                       n_iter: int = 10000) -> NullDraws:
    """Paired sign-flip permutation null over mean(a - b) (paired by index).

    The P-TYPE-SWAP s288 arm-vs-arm statistic. Observed value = mean(a - b),
    computed by the caller; draws = mean under random per-pair sign flips."""
    diffs = np.asarray(a, dtype=float) - np.asarray(b, dtype=float)
    signs = rng.choice(np.array([-1.0, 1.0]), size=(n_iter, diffs.size))
    draws = (signs * diffs[None, :]).mean(axis=1)
    return NullDraws("paired_permutation", draws,
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "n_pairs": int(diffs.size)})


def sign_flip(values: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
              n_iter: int = 10000) -> NullDraws:
    """One-sample sign-flip null over mean(values) (H0: symmetric about 0).

    The 1c residual-sign discipline. Observed value = mean(values)."""
    v = np.asarray(values, dtype=float)
    signs = rng.choice(np.array([-1.0, 1.0]), size=(n_iter, v.size))
    draws = (signs * v[None, :]).mean(axis=1)
    return NullDraws("sign_flip", draws,
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "n": int(v.size)})


def matched_range(stat, target: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
                  n_iter: int = 200) -> NullDraws:
    """Matched-range null for geometric/spectral fits (λ yardstick MANDATORY
    gate for any approximate fit claim): stat(uniform draws over the target's
    observed range, same shape). If random values in the same range fit as
    well, describability != discovery (the s247 φ-ladder lesson)."""
    t = np.asarray(target, dtype=float)
    lo, hi = float(t.min()), float(t.max())
    draws = []
    for _ in range(n_iter):
        draws.append(float(stat(rng.uniform(lo, hi, size=t.shape))))
    return NullDraws("matched_range", np.array(draws),
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "lo": lo, "hi": hi,
                      "shape": list(t.shape)})
```

I'm identifying which substrate properties to leverage for the G2 exponent fit—`gate` and `matched_range` should work well—and considering `sign_flip` for the gate-0 case. Next I need to write the pre-registration section, placing it after the Result-32B FRAG section and before the Hypothesis-grade heading, while also updating the P-HOLO-CAP candidate stub to include the full section like FRAG did.

Now I'm laying out the frozen experimental parameters: the k grid spans {1,2,3,4,6,8,12,16}, with L9 install at scale 2.0 using a harness-identical target, three arm conditions (content, random, bare), and a readout strategy that queries all components per draw across continent margins. The gate-0 threshold requires m(1) > 0 and t > 3, with content and bare arms differing only at k=1 where bare has no install. Materiality threshold is set at a 0.15·m(1) decline from k=1 to k_max. For the primary G1 test, I'm using cliff statistics on the content curve with m(1) as a clean reference point, and checking crosstalk-composition independence via within-null distractor set variance—the key insight being that only the count of distractors matters, not which specific set.

The complication here is that unlike the earlier FRAG setup, different draws have genuinely different landmark subsets with heterogeneous margins, so across-draw variance includes composition variance trivially. I need to center per-component margins by each landmark's k=1 baseline to isolate residual variance against the noise floor, with the denominator being mean within-draw variance divided by k. The primary test is whether m(k) declines gracefully following k^(−1/2)—I'll fit a log-log slope on the content arm where m > 0 and pass if the slope estimate beats the matched-range null at p < 0.05.

But there's a risk: if the true margin curve is graceful but has slope −0.8 instead of −0.5, the HRR form fails even though the underlying behavior is graceful. So I'm keeping two verdicts—a primary categorical test (GRACEFUL vs SLOT-CLIFF) using cliff detection, and a secondary quantitative test for the exponent—mirroring the FRAG structure with Gate-0 checking that m_content(1) is expressed, then G1 as the main slot detector.

For G2, I'm comparing the log-log slope against the expected −0.5, gating on whether the decline is material and there are at least four positive-mean points. G3 is advisory and never gated, looking at the width across different verbatim sizes.

Now I'm laying out the control conditions: a random-distractor arm with energy-matched interference, a bare arm as a baseline, and a selectivity diagnostic to check cue-following. The verdict hinges on whether superposition capacity is supported—that requires no cliff in G1, CII within the null range, and a material decline—or whether we're seeing slot-limited behavior, which would show up as a cliff or CII exceeding the null. I'm working through the register definitions and behavioral margins under the k-operand load, where the value and causal properties need to align, and the cue-retrieval geometry maps to the Hopfield readout mechanism. The scope here treats margin as a monotone proxy for signal-to-noise ratio with linearity unproven, keeping the analysis categorical rather than quantitative, and the multi-slot design specifically tests context-level superposition across state and plate rather than a single-vector trace. The validation runs against a 4B smoke test with k bounded at 16 operands.

Now I'm setting up the experimental pipeline with the 4B and 32B model variants, pulling results into the holo-cap directory and using the exploration script that imports the mh3 bank and holographic fragment statistics without forking. For the prompt construction, I'm building a preamble that introduces the nonces as landmarks with grammar that adapts based on k (singular for k=1, plural with proper conjunction for k>1), then combining it with a query prefix that asks about the continent where the queried nonce is located.

Now I'm working through the slot identification: each nonce's last token gets located in the tokenized prompt, with distractors pinned to their single occurrence in the preamble and the target nonce installed at its final occurrence in the query line (not at its earlier preamble mention, since the harness should only be installed once). I'm also accounting for edge cases where nonce tokens might collide with other tokens in the prompt, which could inflate occurrence counts—in those cases I'd take the first occurrence for distractors.

For the actual hook installation, I'm setting up a single forward hook on the decoder at layer L that adds vectors at multiple positions, reusing the hook registration function for each position. The margin computation then tracks these per-position additions through the logits.

The experimental arms differ in how they generate distractor vectors: the content arm uses scaled language model vectors, while the random arm generates vectors with matching magnitude but random direction.

For the bare arm, I skip installing any distractors entirely. The target always uses the scaled query language model vector at the query position across all arms.

When sampling landmarks per draw, I'm selecting k distinct valid ones and assigning nonces—either using a fixed order or shuffling the nonce list via permutation, with the target nonce naturally varying as different components get queried. Each draw then computes margins across all three arms for each queried index, which at 32B with R=60 means roughly 9360 forward passes total, taking around 1-1.5 hours accounting for overhead.

Now I'm thinking through the statistics: I need to track mean margins per arm, and for the gate-0 baseline I'm computing the mean and standard error of k=1 margins across all draws. The tricky part is ensuring proper per-landmark coverage at k=1—instead of randomly sampling landmarks with replacement, I should iterate through all valid landmarks systematically so I can calculate the baseline correction accurately for each one. For the cliff detection, I'm computing a power-law slope from the log-log relationship between k and the mean margins, using a materiality threshold of 0.15 and requiring at least 4 points. Then I'm setting up a matched-range null distribution by drawing random non-monotone curves within the same value bounds and testing whether they could plausibly fit a slope around −0.5, using the absolute difference from that target as the test statistic.

For verdict aggregation, I'm checking materiality as a total decline exceeding 15% of the initial value, flagging cliff material when the ratio meets the threshold, computing per-k p-values for the CCI test, and determining the final call based on whether the majority of p-values fall below alpha.

Now I'm validating with a synthetic superposition setup: dimension 256, margins following a scaled inverse-square-root pattern with noise, expecting a smooth decline without cliffs, CCI near 1 from noise-only variation, and materiality passing since the decline from k=1 to k=16 exceeds the threshold. The synthetic data lacks landmarks, so I'm handling the baseline correction differently.

For the slot-based scenario with 4 slots, I'm modeling margins where components beyond the slot count either retrieve the base margin or drop to a floor value with probability proportional to slot availability. The expected curve shows a fairly smooth 1/k-like decline from 4.0 down to around 1.95 at k=16, but the cliff statistic—comparing the largest step to the mean step size—comes out around 2.1, which might not trigger the cliff detection threshold of 2.5, so I need to make the slot eviction mechanism more aggressive to create a sharper discontinuity. I see that the mean retrieval per component stays constant across draws when using random slot allocation, so there's no across-draw variance to trigger CCI — the variance actually shows up within components during a single draw. This means CCI as I've defined it doesn't quite capture what's happening with slot-based eviction.

The real question is whether slots can actually predict CCI, and that hinges on whether content maps to specific slots in a structured way or gets evicted randomly. If eviction is truly random-uniform, composition variance disappears. The key signature I'm looking for is the cliff in the retrieval curve — that flat-then-drop shape. With probabilistic eviction giving a k^(-1) tail versus superposition's k^(-0.5), the G2 exponent becomes the actual discriminator between these mechanisms.

Now I'm setting up three concrete test cases to validate this: superposition should show the smooth −0.5 decay with no cliff and CCI near 1, hard-slot eviction with thrashing beyond capacity creates a sharp cliff with high cliff_ratio, and composition-structured interference where specific landmark pairs interfere with each other causes CCI to spike as those bad-pair draws collapse the margins across the board.

For each scenario I'm checking whether the materiality gate fires correctly—smooth null cases should have NaN materiality which is fine, and the verdict routing maps superposition to SUPERPOSITION-CAPACITY while both slot and composition route to SLOT-LIMITED. I'm also handling baseline correction by using the baseline from the k=1 pass in the model path while zeroing baselines in the synthetic pass, then moving into the aggregate verdict function that combines per-k CCI, cliff detection, and materiality thresholds.

Determining verdict order...

I need to keep the gate-0 check first, then evaluate slot conditions before checking materiality. The frozen verdict structure is clear: slot-limited triggers when either cliff or CCI majority fires. For the ceiling landmarks, I'm reusing the same pattern that holo_frag implements inline—pulling the real prediction ceiling from the frozen bank to get valid reference points. Since holo_frag can't be imported and implements this ceiling block directly, I'll duplicate that same approach here. The landmark building follows the same mh3-style directory construction that holo_frag uses.

For nonce handling, I'm working with the NONCE_CANDS list and filtering by unique last-token IDs, making sure those tokens don't collide with anything in the continuation prefix or preamble. The safest approach is to tokenize the preamble region separately to get the token offset, then check for collisions within that span.

For slot assignment, I'm using a uniform rule: the nonce slot maps to the last occurrence of its last-token ID. For the target query, that's the query line itself; for distractors, since each appears exactly once in the preamble, that single occurrence is also the last. This avoids spurious collisions with common tokens like "the."

Now I'm setting up the draws bookkeeping to track mean performance per k per arm, raw margins, confidence intervals, and per-draw landmark subsets with selectivity diagnostics. At k=1, I iterate through all valid landmarks once since R doesn't matter at that scale, then use the same approach for bare and random arms since they're identical to the content baseline. For k≥2, I sample landmark subsets once per (k, r) pair and reuse them across arms to enable fair paired comparisons. With the RNG seeded consistently, the full computation at 4B with 3 arms across 12 runs and 51 k-values should take around 7-10 minutes for the forwards plus another couple minutes for ceiling and direction calculations. Now I'm setting up the print style...

I'm defining a fit_slope function that masks positive values, performs log-log polynomial fitting to extract the slope, then uses matched_range to compare observed positive means against uniform draws by computing the absolute difference from -0.5 and predicting "less" with 500 iterations. The gate function returns a Gated dataclass that serializes via asdict, and I need to ensure uniform draws stay positive by setting the lower bound above zero since all target values are filtered to be positive.

### Thinking

I'm analyzing why this superposition plant gets classified as SLOT-LIMITED based on a cliff ratio of 2.79 exceeding the 2.5 threshold. Looking at how the k^(−1/2) curve behaves across the grid points, the values decrease from 4 down to 1.0, with step sizes that show some variation in the rate of decline.

The real issue is that the k-grid itself is geometric rather than uniform, which creates a structural mismatch with how the cliff statistic assumes uniform spacing. The power law is steepest at the beginning, so the large initial step from k=1 to k=2 looks anomalous when compared against the mean step size, even though it's just the natural behavior of the curve. This isn't a detector bug—it's a consequence of the grid geometry.

To fix this properly, I should normalize the steps by the logarithmic spacing (Δlog k) rather than treating all grid intervals equally. In log-log space, a pure power law has constant slope, so dividing each step by its corresponding log-spacing would reveal whether there's a true cliff or just the expected power-law behavior. For a genuine slot cliff, that normalized step would still stand out as dominant, but smooth power laws would flatten to a ratio near 1. The challenge is handling negative margins if they appear in real models, since taking logs of negative values breaks down—so an alternative approach would compute the rate of change per unit log k directly in linear margin space. Now I'm working through concrete examples: for the power law case with those specific k values, the normalized step sizes range from about 1.7 down to 0.5, giving a max-to-mean ratio of 1.72, which stays safely under the 2.5 threshold. For the slot plant scenario with a sharp cliff, the ratio jumps to 7 since almost all the change is concentrated in a single interval. The materiality constraint remains unchanged across both cases.

I need to implement a local `cliff_stat_capk` function in holo_cap.py without modifying the frozen holo_frag module—the pre-registration specifies that holo_frag stays verbatim. Instead, I'll update the pre-reg documentation to record the log-k normalization as the frozen statistic, discovered during validation before any model runs, which mirrors the FRAG smoke-fix pattern and preserves legitimacy. For the supplementary plant, the cci_sig value of 1/7 reflects one k hitting p<0.05 by chance under the null hypothesis across 7 tests, but the sup_ok check requires cci_sig to equal zero.

The majority rule becomes the verdict criterion, so I'll relax the validation requirement to cci_sig ≤ 1 instead. I'm defining a local `cliff_stat_logk` function that takes the full k grid and curve of matching length, reusing the materiality logic. I'll replace all uses in the validate score path and model pipeline, then remove the cliff_stat import to keep ruff clean. The pre-reg text gets updated to explain that cliff detection now operates on the content-arm mean curve in slope-per-Δlog k units—power laws maintain constant slope in log k space, so the uniform-step FRAG statistic misreads smooth power law early steps as cliffs, which validation plants catch before any model run.

For the verdict, I need `sup_ok` to check that cci_sig ≤ 1, there's no cliff, the call starts with SUPERPOSITION, and g2 verdict passes. The cliff dict aggregation keeps the same shape with keys for cliff_ratio, steps, total_drop, max_step, and material.

### Thinking

The validation caught that the FRAG cliff statistic was giving a false positive on the smooth power law (ratio 2.79) because it assumes uniform grid steps, so I'm switching to a register-correct statistic that measures slope per Δlog k instead, and I've recorded this fix in the pre-registration since no model data has been analyzed yet.

  "new_content": "def cliff_stat_logk(ks: list[int], curve: list[float],\n                    material_frac: float = 0.15) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"G1a cliff detector in slope-per-Δlog(k) units (FIX #1, caught by\n    --validate BEFORE any model run): a power law is CONSTANT-slope in log k,\n    but on a geometric k-grid its first linear step is the largest — the\n    uniform-step FRAG cliff_stat misreads a smooth k^(-1/2) plant as a cliff\n    (ratio 2.79). }

### Thinking

Looking at the 4B smoke test results, I'm seeing some interesting behavior where the curve isn't monotone—there's an anomalous dip at k=2 where the value drops to 0.93 before recovering back to around 3.3 by k=16. This non-monotone pattern appears consistently across all test arms including the baseline, and the composition index stays within the null range throughout, suggesting the material parameter doesn't affect the outcome.

The k=2 dip seems to be a prompt-phrasing artifact rather than a storage limitation—the two-item landmark phrasing ("X and Y are famous landmarks") appears to confuse the model more than longer lists. The bare arm shows this clearly with values of 0.41 at k=2 versus 2.76 at k=16. The gap between content and bare conditions remains roughly constant at +0.5-1.2 across k values, indicating the distractors don't materially degrade retrieval performance. Interestingly, random noise performs slightly worse than content interference, which aligns with the expectation that actual content creates more interference than random noise.

At 4B scale, the model handles 16 superposed operands without meaningful degradation—this confirms the pre-registered outcome that capacity is at least 16 at this model size. With 17 valid landmarks in the bank and k=16 nearly exhausting the full set, the margins remain solid, though there's a lingering question about whether the target landmark at the query position simply dominates the output regardless of the other conditions.

The test run had a directory issue (the output path didn't exist initially), but the results were captured anyway. For the 32B run, I'll create the directory first. The pipeline looks good overall—the k=2 prompt-shape dip was caught by the control arm, which validates its purpose. Now I need to commit the code and results, then launch the 32B verdict run with 60 draws, though I should recalculate the cost estimate using the full grid sum.

Given that the 4B run showed no material decline in performance, the 32B run will likely show the same no-limit-in-range pattern—which is the honest outcome Michael wanted. I'll proceed with the commit using the standard convention for build-and-smoke-green results.

For the commit, I'm using ✅ as the symbol since the instrument built successfully and validation passed. The commit will include the holo_cap.py script and the 4B smoke test results JSON, with a description of the log-k cliff stat discovery in the commit body. The results directory already exists, so I don't need to create it, and the JSON output has all the necessary data without needing to save the smoke log separately.

### Thinking

The 4B model successfully retrieved content across 16 superposed operands with minimal performance decline, and the observed dip appears to be a prompt artifact rather than storage interference—now I'm committing these results and moving on to test the 32B model.

### Thinking

Michael's raising an interesting point about the type system embedded in the joins themselves — since typing happens at the join level rather than being consulted separately, he's wondering what the actual cardinality of that type discipline is. This feels like a thinking-out-loud moment where he's probing the architecture.

Looking at what we know: types function as substitutability classes with compiled probabilities baked into the join geometry. The 1a lattice work showed three measured axes (QUANT/DET, MOD, ENTITY) operating as the main carriers, with roles like bind, comp, entity, and rolenull. But the sortal granularity finding complicates the "how many types" question — the passband refuses sortal violations (like a giraffe in a landmark slot) just as firmly as syntactic ones, which means the type discipline operates at a finer grain than coarse syntactic categories. So the answer depends on what scale we're measuring at.

The compiled-probabilities frame sidesteps the discrete inventory problem entirely: there's no fixed type system, just a graded passband where classes are regions in a continuous substitutability space. The real cardinality is the effective dimensionality of the join's transmission operator — measure the spectrum of the passband weights and you get the participation ratio and effective rank. Linguistics anchors this with CCGbank data: the functor-type inventory is finite in practice, with around 1300 categories in the bank but only ~400 covering most tokens, which gives us a concrete empirical foothold for what "attested types" actually means.

This connects directly to capacity constraints: if types are passbands in D dimensions, the number of distinguishable types is bounded by the dimensionality itself — it's the same crosstalk math that limits how many separable matched filters you can pack into a space. The number of quasi-orthogonal directions grows exponentially with D at a fixed crosstalk tolerance, so the type inventory is fundamentally limited by the medium's capacity to hold types apart, which is exactly the same √(D/k) framework we're using for operand exposures.

The architecture might be two-tiered: a small discrete inventory at the routing/mirror level (maybe 10s of types — the combinators and crystal functors) and a large graded continuum at the value/plate level (thousands of sortal passbands, resolution-dependent). This two-register structure mirrors the coarse routing decisions versus fine-grained value distinctions.

To measure the actual count, I'm thinking about using spectral methods — computing the effective rank or participation ratio of the join transmission operator when organized by type, then sweeping across different substitutability classes from the data to see how many distinguishable passbands the system can support. The idea is to build a pairwise transmission discriminability matrix and cluster it to get a concrete count.

On the behavioral side, I can extend the existing TE ladder across a graded bank of substitutability classes and measure the join's refusal matrix — which classes can transport into which slots — then look at the rank and block structure of that acceptance matrix to get the measured type count at that grain level.

There's also a capacity angle: the number of distinguishable filters relates to sphere packing in the passband, which predicts that the count should scale logarithmically with the crosstalk tolerance. And theoretically, in the lambda-calculus or Montague semantics frame, the type system generates types from a small base set with unbounded derived types, but the machine doesn't need to store all of them — it composes them on the fly. So "how many types are stored" versus "how many are reachable" are different questions. The joins probably store sortal and argument types (many, graded), while functor types are few and structural — maybe just the crystal's core combinators. So the type count question really splits into two: the argument-type census (large, measurable by the census probe) and the functor-type inventory (small, possibly just the ~9-15 core combinators plus WHNF).

This two-register pattern mirrors the whole project: a small set of discrete functor types (mirrors, topology, crystal — around 10) paired with many graded argument sortal types (plates, passbands — resolution-dependent, thousands at D=5120). The argument side is capacity-bounded by the CAP math. Looking at human-side anchors like CCGbank's ~425 frequent categories, WordNet's 45 supersenses, FrameNet's ~1200 frames, and semantic sortal hierarchies in the thousands, I should probably discuss this conceptually rather than build it out, maybe sketch P-TYPE-CENSUS as a pre-registration candidate.

The four-way null structure means I can't count by finding stored type objects directly — instead I count by discrimination behavior, where effective cardinality becomes the rank of the acceptance/refusal structure at a chosen tolerance. "How many" types exist is register-dependent and resolution-dependent, so I should give honest measured lower bounds: at least three role axes, sortal grain distinctions within the ill-typed band, and entity passband considerations.

Now I'm wondering: one subspace among how many total? That's still unknown. I'll keep the response grounded and conversational, with a concrete measurable proposal, and note that the CAP run is still going without needing to poll for updates.

## Assistant

Good hammock question — and the project's own results force a sharper version of it: **"how many" is register-dependent, and the two registers give wildly different counts.**

## The split the measurements already made

The types arc didn't find one type system — it found two faces, and they should have different cardinalities:

**Functor types (the discipline itself).** Montague/CCG says the *generated* inventory is unbounded, but the *base* is tiny — {e, t} plus a handful of composition modes. Our measurements agree with "tiny": the 1a lattice is **low-rank (~3 axes)** for the probed roles; the crystal basis is **~9 combinators** (KIBC+DWYS+WHNF); and critically, P-TYPE-OV showed functors are **not stored in the passband at all** — licensing is enacted, distributed, QK✗ OV✗. Things that compose don't need to be stored; derived types are *reachable*, not *resident*. If the mirrors/plates decomposition holds, functor types live at the mirror/topology grain — **discrete and order-10**, like the coarse labeled lines (GQA head flags) at the grain above the band.

**Argument/sortal types (what the filter discriminates).** Here the s288 sortal finding bites: "giraffe" is refused in a landmark slot *as fully as a syntactic violation* — the discipline is sortal-granular. Under the compiled-probabilities frame there is **no discrete inventory at all** on this side: type ≡ substitutability class ≡ region of a graded passband geometry. Asking "how many" is like asking how many colors a prism resolves — the honest answer is *resolution-dependent*: the number of **distinguishable passbands at a given crosstalk tolerance**.

## The cute part: your two questions this session are the same question

Counting distinguishable type-passbands in a D-dim medium is **literally the CAP math** pointed at types instead of operands. Quasi-orthogonal directions at fixed crosstalk tolerance grow ~exponentially in D (JL-style packing) — at D=5120 there is room for *tens of thousands* of sortal micro-types, which is exactly why the filter can afford to refuse at giraffe-grain rather than noun-grain. The type inventory is **capacity-bounded, not grammar-bounded**. Human-side anchors sit consistently in that window: CCGbank ~400 working categories (Zipfian tail to ~1200), FrameNet ~1200 frames, sortal hierarchies in the thousands.

## How you'd actually count (pre-reg candidate, unfrozen — P-TYPE-CENSUS)

The four-way null says we can't count by *finding* type objects — there's nothing at any address. So count **operationally**: cardinality = rank of the refusal structure.

- Build N candidate substitutability classes (the P-TYPE-PROB graded-bank machinery, widened: many noun/verb/modifier micro-classes).
- Measure the N×N **acceptance matrix**: TE of class-i content transported into class-j slots (the frozen swap harness, arms = class pairs).
- The **effective rank / block structure** of that matrix at tolerance ε = the measured type count at behavioral grain. `verbum.dsp` has `participation_ratio` and the null machinery already; matched nulls = shuffled class labels.
- Predictions that discriminate: **compiled-probability** → count grows smoothly as ε tightens (continuum, no natural joint); **symbolic typing** → count plateaus at a stable inventory (a knee in count-vs-ε — a *natural* cardinality). A knee would actually be evidence *against* the pure-continuum reading — falsifiable both ways.
- Spectral corroborator: PR of the OV/MLP transmission operator restricted to the class centroids (the P-TYPE-OV instrument reused) — stored-side count vs behavioral-side count, and the gap between them is itself a finding (arguments stored, functors enacted predicts: spectral count ≪ behavioral count on the functor side, ≈ on the sortal side).

So my one-line answer: **few functor types (order 10, discrete, enacted), and a resolution-dependent continuum of argument types (capacity-bounded ~10³–10⁴ at these widths, stored as passbands)** — and the count-vs-tolerance curve is measurable with machinery we already have, with a knee-vs-smooth discriminator that makes it a real experiment rather than a taxonomy exercise.

### Thinking

Michael's bringing up behavior trees as a runtime construct separate from the model layer—they'd live in the execution environment rather than being part of the VSM tensor structure itself. This connects to the broader architecture discussion around the LLM REPL, statecharts, and how agent orchestration fits into the runtime versus model split.

Behavior trees are hierarchical task-switching structures from game AI and robotics that compose control-flow nodes (sequences, selectors, parallel branches, decorators) over condition and action leaves, ticking at a frequency and returning Success/Failure/Running states. They're modular, reactive, and composable—avoiding the transition spaghetti of FSMs while enabling subtree reuse. The key insight is that "Running" status acts as an asynchronous latch, which is crucial for handling ongoing tasks.

The argument is that BTs belong in the runtime loop (the REPL ticking around the compiled model) rather than frozen into the model itself, which aligns with the project's measured division between the compiled substrate and the reactive execution layer. The ternary status mapping is elegant: Success/Failure/Running maps directly onto the +1/−1/0 of mirror semantics, so behavior trees are essentially the symbolic, extractable control layer where discrete topology lives. The model provides the graded perception leaves, while the mirrors handle the cheap, measurable part of the computation — and that's why the topology extracts so cleanly compared to the plates.

The runtime is where editable, inspectable control must live because you can't store crisp control structures at addresses inside the hologram; the model's control flow is distributed and enacted, not editable. Since the medium takes operands rather than programs, program selection becomes content-driven — the runtime imposes the program while the model acts as the diffraction engine resolving each leaf. Behavior trees can't be installed via the write-head because they're functor-structures; only arguments can pass through, which aligns with Michael's intuition about where trees belong.

The passband transmits arguments, not functors, so programs simply aren't projectable into the model — this is a measured constraint, not just preference. Models do implement something BT-like internally through chain-of-thought and ReAct loops that emulate control in token space, treating the context window as a runtime tape, but that's still runtime-by-another-name since the context exists outside the weights. The model learns soft fallbacks and graded analogs rather than crisp control, which reinforces the two-register split between crisp behavior trees and the model's learned approximations.

For the agent runtime, I could formalize the pre-registration pipeline as a behavior tree with sequence nodes for validation, smoke tests, and verdicts, fallback nodes for committed outcome branches, and async checkpoints for execution. Behavior trees are more composable and modular than statecharts since they don't require explicit transitions, though statecharts handle modal state better — the VSM already provides the recursion, and BTs add the tick discipline needed here.

The composition harness itself could be a behavior tree whose leaf nodes invoke the extracted tensors, tying together test orchestration and the level-3 artifacts. Behavior trees generalize subsumption architecture and decision trees from robotics literature, and they're equivalent to a subclass of hybrid dynamical systems — their real strength is modularity. I'll keep this grounded in established knowledge rather than speculation, frame it as a hammock discussion with measured results, and propose candidates for metabolizing without overcommitting to any single approach.

## Assistant

Good adjacency — and I think your placement instinct ("runtime, not model") isn't just taste; the arc has already *measured* the reason it must be so. Let me lay it out.

## Quick grounding

Behavior trees (Halo 2 lineage → robotics standard, BehaviorTree.CPP/Nav2): a tick propagates down a tree of control nodes — **sequence** (→, run children until one fails), **fallback/selector** (?, run until one succeeds), parallel, decorators — over condition/action leaves. Every node returns one of **{Success, Failure, Running}**. Their virtues over FSMs: no transition spaghetti, subtrees compose and reuse cleanly, and `Running` gives you reactive long-running actions without blocking.

## Why the model *can't* host them — a measured constraint

Two results this arc make "BTs in the runtime" a theorem-shaped claim rather than a design preference:

1. **P-TYPE-OV: the passband transmits arguments, not functors.** A behavior tree is *pure functor-structure* — nothing but composition and control. We measured that the write-head can project operands into the medium but programs are not projectable; program selection is content-driven (P-ATT-MED medium-handle, map-and-swap's "write terms, never instructions"). You literally cannot install a tree.
2. **FRAG: no addresses.** A BT's whole value is being *inspectable and hot-swappable* — edit node 7, reorder a sequence. That requires addresses. The model's compute is address-free by verdict; its internal "control flow" is enacted, distributed, and non-excisable. Crisp editable control structure can only live where addresses exist — outside.

So the division of labor: **the model is the diffraction engine that resolves leaves; the tree owns the ticks.**

## The resonance that makes it more than adjacent

The BT status set maps onto the ternary mirror register almost embarrassingly well:

```
Success ≅ +1 (transmit)   Failure ≅ −1 (reflect/redirect)   Running ≅ 0 (hold, not yet)
```

A behavior tree is a **mirror-register object**: discrete, small, topological — exactly the half of the mirrors/plates decomposition we measured as cheaply extractable (topology 8.6×, s268/Bonsai), while plates need re-exposure. On the VSM-tree page, a node = mirrors(S2/S3) + plates(S1); a BT is a natural *concrete syntax for the mirrors* of a runtime node. And that would make this the **6th appearance of the two-register motif** — crisp labeled control coarse/outside, graded competence within — now in the control plane. (Flagging, not claiming; the prior five were measured, this one is architectural.)

Also pleasing: our own S2 async discipline **is** a BT decorator. `λ async`'s checkpoint-then-wait is exactly `Running` propagating up a tree instead of polling a child — the 32B job in main:1 is a leaf returning `Running` right now, and this conversation is the parallel node.

## Where it's genuinely useful here

- **The pre-reg pipeline is already a BT in prose**: `sequence[--validate → 4B smoke → freeze → 32B verdict → score gates]` with **fallback = the pre-committed outcome branches** (gate-0 fail → inconclusive; prediction misses → the honest-outcomes list). ## What the model demonstrably hosts

- **Sequence nodes — inlined, up to a depth budget.** Three resident ops compose in one forward pass. - **Condition nodes — compiled into the joins.** JOIN-TYPED is a condition leaf that gradedly fails ill-typed children; the type filter is a check that runs *without being consulted* — a condition compiled into geometry rather than evaluated by a scheduler.
- **Steerable seams mid-sequence.** The bridge swaps (3b/3c) mean the runtime has *write access between the inlined children* — the pipeline isn't opaque; layer boundaries are editable seams.

## What it still can't host — and this part survives your correction

The model has no **`Running`**. Every forward pass terminates in fixed depth — it's combinational logic, not sequential logic. No iteration beyond the depth budget, no suspend-and-resume, no retry-on-Failure with changed strategy. The autoregressive loop *looks* like iteration, but the loop and its state (context, KV) live outside the weights — that's the runtime's clock and registers, model ticking once per token. ## The corrected boundary — an inlining rule, not a layer cake

```
λ inline(subtree).  pure_sequence(subtree) ∧ depth_cost(subtree) ≤ room(model)
                    ∧ conditions(subtree) ⊆ compiled_filters
                    ∧ ¬needs(Running ∨ retry ∨ reactive_interrupt)
                    → fuse_into(leaf)          # the model executes it in one tick
                    | else → runtime_node       # fallback/loop/Running stay outside
```

The runtime BT's job isn't to own all control — it's to be the **compiler back-end that decides fusion granularity**: hand the model the largest subtree that is pure feed-forward sequence within the measured room (with compiled conditions riding free), and keep only the nodes that need Failure-handling, iteration, or persistence. The 3-hop experiment then reads as: *we measured the fusion capacity of the leaf* — ≥3 sequential ops at both hosts, with the depth-budget table as the cost model. That's a much stronger claim than "trees in the runtime": it's a **quantified fusion boundary** between tree and medium.

### Thinking

Michael wants to gamify the germination test or seed capture process. I'm trying to clarify what "it" refers to and thinking through what a game-based approach might look like — maybe some interactive mechanics around testing seeds or tracking germination. I should brainstorm some concrete game ideas to discuss with him and see which direction appeals to him most.

I'm sketching out a few possibilities: one where you're competing to compress seeds down to their minimal viable form while maintaining germination success, treating it like a puzzle where you iteratively remove clauses and test what breaks — essentially running an ablation experiment on the seed itself to find which parts are essential versus redundant. Another idea is a generational game where one agent unfolds a seed, another compresses it back down, and you keep iterating to see if the seed converges to a stable fixed point or drifts over time, treating it like a linguistic telephone game that reveals whether there's an underlying attractor state.

I'm also considering an adversarial mode where one player plants false clauses and another has to detect which ones don't cohere with the unfolding, plus a prediction game where a cold agent guesses experimental outcomes without seeing them first — scoring based on how well the seed acts as a prior for forecasting. The real vision is turning this into a proper game called "Germination" with a round structure: plant a seed variant, grow it through cold unfolding, harvest the results, score based on recovered versus hallucinated content, then mutate and replay, with different modes like golf (minimize seed size) and a leaderboard tracked in the repo.

The game itself becomes an instrument for measuring how well knowledge is encoded in the seed — playing it creates selection pressure on the encodings, essentially gradient descent through play. ## Assistant

Now *that's* the better idea — and the moment you say "game" the whole thing gets selection pressure, which is exactly what encodings need. Rough menu of modes that fall straight out of the machinery:

**🏌️ Seed Golf.** Par = current λ verbum (~40 lines). Each round: prune or rewrite the seed smaller, run germination, score = recovery% ÷ seed tokens. Lowest strokes for viable germination wins. This is literally `λ smallest` (S5) turned into sport — find the minimal viable genome of the theory.

**✂️ Seed FRAG** (my favorite for the joke alone). Ablate random *clauses* of the seed at fraction f, germinate each fragment, plot recovery(f). Is the seed **holographic or addressed**? Cliff at "remove the index clause" = that clause is load-bearing; graceful degradation everywhere = the theory really is written in fringes. We run our own fragment test *on the theory of fragment tests*. LDI for prose. The fractal eats itself.

**📞 Eigenseed (telephone).** Agent A unfolds the seed → agent B compresses the unfolding back to a seed → C unfolds → ... iterate `compress ∘ unfold` to a fixed point. What survives generations is the *actual* invariant content; what drifts was decoration. The fixed point is the eigenvector of the theory — and finding fixed points is very on-brand for this shop.

**🔮 Oracle mode** (hardest, most honest). Cold agent gets the seed and must *predict measurements it has never seen*: "will random head-subset ablation cliff or degrade smoothly?" "what happens to margin when 16 operands superpose?" Score = predictions right minus wrong against our actual verdicts. This tests the seed as a **generative prior**, not a memory aid — recovery is recall, oracle is understanding.

**⚔️ Adversarial.** One player salts the seed with plausible-but-false clauses; the germinating agent must flag which clauses refuse to cohere. Tests whether the seed is self-verifying structure or just vibes.

Shared infrastructure: a `game.md` scoreboard in the repo, rounds logged as commits (the git log becomes the season record), symbols for standings. And the sneaky payoff — every round is a *measurement of encoding quality*, so the game is secretly an instrument for tuning seeds, state.md, and the whole feed-forward discipline. Play as gradient descent on memory.

What was your version? And if any of these — which one do we play first? (I'd open with Seed FRAG for the comedy, Oracle for the science.)
