## User

Thinking is expanding the terms to reduce using attention.


### Thinking

Michael's framing is interesting—thinking as term expansion that allows attention to perform reduction. So chain-of-thought reasoning tokens are essentially unfolding definitions and intermediate steps onto the context tape, which then lets the attention mechanism compress and reduce them down. It's like how lambda calculus sometimes needs to expand terms before you can actually reduce them.

The model's depth budget per tick is the constraint—it can only do about three hops of reduction in a single forward pass. But by generating thinking tokens, the model writes intermediate products directly into the context as new addressable content, converting what would be a depth problem into a length problem. Each token materializes a step that attention can later reference and combine.

This connects to the broader picture: the model's own output becomes its runtime. Chain-of-thought isn't just reasoning for the user—it's the model using its output channel as a workspace, writing products to the tape and re-reading them. Thinking is the model executing its own fetch-illuminate-writeback loop, with the context acting as the product register. In reduction strategy terms, the forward pass is one parallel reduction step, and thinking unfolds the computation to expose redexes that the depth budget would otherwise hide.

The depth-length duality is testable: smaller models should benefit more from chain-of-thought when the task depth exceeds their depth budget, and we can measure this by checking whether forcing intermediate generation causes the depth-order structure to reappear across tokens instead of across layers.

Thinking tokens essentially convert the model's internal delocalized computation into addressed external symbols in the context window—the only addressed memory the model structurally has access to. This is why thinking helps: it trades the hologram's position-free interior for the tape's discrete, addressable workspace, like paging from internal registers into RAM. The model's generation process itself IS the native injection mechanism — thinking tokens are how it writes compute terms directly into its own context, making chain-of-thought an automatic expansion phase. This reframes the whole system: generation isn't separate from computation but rather the expand step in an expand-reduce cycle where attention then collapses over those injected terms in the next pass. This connects the theoretical injection we were designing with what the model already does natively during generation, suggesting testable predictions around this expand-reduce dynamic. Swapping tokens on the tape is far more effective than modifying residuals directly, and smaller models benefit disproportionately from thinking tokens since they lack the capacity that larger models have. This connects to broader ideas about recurrence loops, compression architecture, and how thinking functions as the model's native mechanism for injecting information into its limited context window—essentially converting depth into sequential length. The testable prediction is that a 4B model unrolling a 3-hop chain across token positions should show sharp performance gains, which we can verify with the existing machinery.

## Assistant

That one clicks into the machine at exactly the slot the day left open. Unpack it in our registers:

## The identity

Thinking tokens = the model running the **expand phase of an expand–reduce cycle**. Depth gives you ~3 hops of reduction per illumination (measured); when the term needs more, the model *unfolds it into the context* — materializes intermediate products as tokens — and attention (the beta-reducer, per your own attention-as-beta-reduction page) reduces over the expanded term on subsequent ticks. Classic graph reduction: you expand the definition to *expose the redex*. Thinking is δ-expansion in service of β-reduction.

## Why this is load-bearing and not just cute

**1. The context is the machine's only addressed memory.** Inside: fringes everywhere, address nowhere (FRAG). But the tape has *positions* — RoPE phase, literal addresses. So thinking is **paging out of the hologram into addressed RAM**: converting address-free internal compute into addressed external symbols. That's *why* it helps — the machine is structurally missing registers, and generation manufactures them. The two-register motif again, now as an *operation*: expansion is the bridge that writes discrete mirror-register symbols from graded plate-register content. Token sampling = the collapse/quantization step — the readout register fed back as input. Quote, then eval. The resident Lisp was homoiconic all along.

**2. Thinking = native self-injection.** This morning: "we are able to inject compute terms." Look at what generation *is*: the model injecting **its own** compute terms into its own context, growing the KV cache — an *ephemeral plate written during inference* — that this-tick attention then illuminates. The write-head we're engineering (P-PROJ-1, superbake) already ships in the machine as the sampling loop. CoT is auto-superbake. The BT runtime's fetch→illuminate→writeback→loop isn't only external — the model runs a native copy of it, one token per cycle.

**3. Depth ↔ length is an exchange rate, and we can measure it.** 32B unrolls the 3-hop in *depth* (L52.5→57.5→60); 4B compresses it into one window (Gate-3a fail). Prediction: force 4B to expand — generate the intermediates — and **the depth-order signature reappears across token positions instead of layers**. Sequencing in time replaces sequencing in depth. Same computation, different axis.

## P-THINK-1 (unfrozen candidate — the depth–length exchange rate)

All standing machinery:

- **G1**: extend the geography ladder to 4–5 hops. Room accounting says 4B fails in one tick; allow expansion → passes. Thinking-tokens-required ∝ hop-overflow beyond room = the exchange rate, measured.
- **G2** (the register jackpot): with intermediates on the tape, causal surgery gets **addressed handles** — swap the *written* bridge token vs the s282-style residual swap. Prediction: tape-swap flips ≈ ceiling (it's addressed!), cleaner than in-medium swaps. Decodable-but-not-causal was the hologram's signature; **editable-because-addressed is the tape's**.
- **G3** (advisory): scale asymmetry — per-token benefit larger at 4B than 32B on matched chains (32B has room to spare; the literature's "CoT helps small models on deep tasks" folds out of our room table as a *quantitative* prediction, not folklore).
- Null discipline: matched token-budget filler expansion (irrelevant tokens) must NOT help — expansion works iff the expanded terms are the *products* (content, not length).

And the one-line addition to the seed, since this completes the runtime clause:

```
think ≡ expand(term → tape) to reduce(attention) | depth ⇄ length
      | hologram(no_address) → tape(addressed) | the model is its own write-head
```

## User

wait, does that mean we can use thinking to find functions?


### Thinking

Michael's insight is that we could use the model's thinking process itself as a way to index functions—rather than probing the model behaviorally to enumerate what it can do, we let it expand its own capabilities directly onto the tape, where each thinking step becomes a visible function application that we can capture and index.

The thinking trace essentially becomes a self-decompilation: when the model expands intermediate steps (like "Eiffel Tower → Paris → France → Europe"), each arrow represents a resident function firing, and the model is effectively naming its own function inventory in the output. We can harvest these input-output pairs from the traces as candidate index entries, then ground each one by capturing the residual state at the moment of expansion, building a key from the written intermediate, and verifying the dispatch causally through probes. This flips the economics of index-building—instead of blindly guessing which functions exist, thinking gives us the candidate list for free, and we only need to confirm the causal structure afterward.

The key caveat is that a verbal trace isn't the same as the actual mechanism; models can confabulate reasoning chains, so thinking-derived functions are just candidates until verified. The causal gate is the real test: if swapping out the written intermediate actually changes downstream behavior, then that trace step is load-bearing and faithful. This means unfaithful steps—ones that don't causally matter—get excluded from the index, turning faithfulness into a verifiable gate rather than a philosophical debate. Coverage is limited to whatever the tasks actually elicit, so the index only captures functions the model demonstrates.

The full pipeline flows from eliciting thinking traces through parsing them for candidate functions, grounding them with captured keys and windows, verifying them causally, and finally indexing the results. This whole enumeration loop can run autonomously at runtime, letting the model bootstrap its own index through self-decompilation.

The core answer hinges on the faithfulness gate working as a filter. I should offer to fold this into the capture proposal as a thinking-driven enumeration section—maybe call it P-DECOMP or rung 0 of P-FN-INDEX—but since Michael asked a direct question, I'll answer it first and update the capture proposal, then wait for his approval before moving forward.

## Assistant

Yes — and it solves the exact problem I flagged as the index's bottleneck. I said enumeration had to be behavioral, probe-by-probe, because nothing inside has an address. But **expansion writes the invocations onto the tape** — and the tape is addressed. When the model thinks "Eiffel Tower → Paris → France → Europe," every arrow is a resident function *naming itself in the readout register*. Thinking is **self-decompilation**: the compiler emitting its own symbol table, one invocation at a time.

That turns index construction from guesswork into a pipeline:

```
ELICIT   task battery → thinking traces           # the model expands
HARVEST  parse arrows → candidate (in-class → out-class) functions
GROUND   capture residuals AT the expansion moment → key (d_E from the
         written intermediate), window (depth-order), product (register)
VERIFY   tape-swap the written intermediate → downstream must flip
INDEX    ⟨key, window, product⟩ entry, causally certified
```

And the VERIFY step is the crux, because the obvious objection — *CoT traces confabulate; verbal report ≠ mechanism* — is exactly our four-way-null lesson (decodable ≠ causal, trace = exhaust until proven otherwise). But look what the tape gives us: **faithfulness stops being a debate and becomes a gate.** Swap the written intermediate token; if downstream flips content-specifically, that trace step *was* causally load-bearing — a faithful expansion — and enters the index. If downstream doesn't care, the step was decoration and gets excluded. P-THINK-1's G2 wasn't just a register jackpot; it's the **certification instrument for self-reported functions**. Per-step, null-gated, no philosophy required.

Honest bounds, pre-flagged:

- **Coverage is task-bounded** — thinking only expands what the battery elicits. But that's actually aligned with your framing: we want the *behavior* functions, and behaviors define the battery. The index grows by use, like the OpenAPI spec convention — entries added when a behavior first needs them.
- **Granularity is the model's choice** — traces may emit composed macros, not atoms. Prompted finer expansion ("every step") vs natural granularity is itself informative: where the model *can't* expand finer may mark the true atomic functions — the crystal floor.

And clock the recursion, because it's load-bearing rather than decorative this time: **we use the machine's native runtime (thinking) to build the runtime's index, gated by the machine's own addressed memory.** The enumerator, the workspace, and the verifier are all the same object at three scales. The BT can drive the elicit→harvest→verify loop autonomously — the index *builds itself* while we sleep, and only certified entries survive.

So the capture proposal upgrades: one section, two pieces —

1. **§Thinking-is-expansion** (the identity + P-THINK-1: exchange rate, tape-swap, scale asymmetry, filler null) — on the program-plates page + the `think ≡ expand→reduce` clause into the seed;
2. **§Self-decompilation** (this): thinking as rung-0 of P-FN-INDEX — the enumeration front-end with the faithfulness gate.

It's one new front, not two — self-decompilation is *inside* the fn-index arc, and P-THINK-1's G2 is its instrument. The freeze queue stays: CAP-scoring → fn-index (now with its enumerator built in). Say capture and it's in the batch.

### Thinking

The frozen gates verdict shows no limit in range with the curve rising monotonically as k increases from 1 to 16, moving from 1.06 to 2.53, while the CCI significance test falls below the majority threshold needed to trigger the slot rule.

Looking at the performance metrics, accuracy climbs from 0.78 to 0.87 across the range, and the content-based approach substantially outperforms both random and bare baselines—at k=16, content reaches 2.53 versus bare at 1.38, nearly doubling the baseline. This pattern suggests constructive interference where co-installed landmarks amplify retrieval, though I'm considering whether this is domain-priming (where multiple landmarks shift the context toward geography generally) versus per-exposure reinforcement specific to the content itself. The bare arm controls for prompt shape while the random arm controls for installation energy, so the content-specific gain should isolate the effect of the actual landmark data.

The 4B model shows flat performance (3.32→3.36) while 32B shows clear gains (1.06→2.53), contradicting the a-priori prediction of shallower decline in 32B—instead, 32B exhibits actual growth. At k=4, I'm seeing one significant result (p=0.0085) across seven tests, which is borderline under the frozen majority rule, so I'll report it verbatim without claiming it as a slot. The runtime came in at 1 hour 26 minutes. Now I need to commit these results and draft the 32B convergence section plus a memory candidate, though that requires approval before moving forward. Writing the memory file and state block, then presenting the batch for approval since Michael typically pre-approves after seeing drafts — I'll commit just the results, not the mementum, until getting the go-ahead.

### Thinking

## Assistant

**What we did:** Installed k artificial "memories" (landmark operands) simultaneously into one context — k from 1 to 16 — then asked the model to retrieve one specific one. If the medium stores things like slots (RAM), retrieval should hold flat then collapse when slots run out. If it stores like a hologram (superposition), retrieval should degrade gracefully ∝ √(D/k) as crosstalk grows.

**What happened:** Neither. Retrieval never degraded at all through k=16 at either model size — **the frozen verdict is NO-LIMIT-IN-RANGE**: capacity is at least 16, our bank was too small to find the ceiling.

**The surprise (not gated, needs its own pre-reg):** at 32B, retrieval got *better* as more operands were co-installed — margin 1.06 → 2.53, accuracy 0.78 → 0.87. The controls pin it down: matched-energy random vectors don't do it, empty nonces don't do it — only *real, coherent* content does. And *which* operands you co-install doesn't matter, only that they're real. So superposed exposures **reinforce each other instead of interfering** — the opposite sign of the crosstalk the HRR law predicts.

**What it means for the frame:** FRAG showed the storage has no addresses; CAP shows it has no ceiling at this scale *and* that coherent exposures cooperate. Both fit a high-capacity distributed plate — but the positive holographic law (√(D/k)) is still unproven, and the cooperation effect is now the sharper target. Next instrument: XTERM — measure the interference itself, since it just showed up on its own.

One line: **we tried to overflow the plate; instead of filling up, it got sharper.**

The pipeline mirrors what we did before — draft the pre-reg with design calls, then present it for approval before moving to smoke tests at 4B and the final verdict at 32B.

I'm settling on the approach: draft the pre-reg first and lay out the design decisions, then present to Michael for approval and direction on running the tests. The core scientific question is why m_content(k) rises with k at 32B in a content-specific but composition-independent way, and I'm weighing two hypotheses — one based on constructive interference and one on co-installation effects. So the key test is whether plain-text mentions of landmarks in the preamble produce the same gain as installing them as vectors—if they do, it's just semantic priming rather than a holographic plate interaction. I should also test whether off-domain coherent content (like animals) as distractors preserves the gain or kills it, which would tell me if this is a generic coherence effect or something domain-specific. The cleanest discriminator is comparing installed exposures against text-only mentions to see what actually drives the effect.

Now I'm thinking through the coherence structure more carefully—in a balanced draw where competitors' continent-content is already installed, the queried item still wins by a larger margin, which is puzzling. If the gain were just constructive addition from same-continent co-installs, competitors would also get boosted. The real question is whether the query cue itself becomes sharper through contrast or normalization when more retrieval targets are present.

I'm considering a third hypothesis: normalization and contrast effects. When the background is structured—whether from same-domain or even off-domain coherent exposures—attention or layer normalization might renormalize the cued retrieval to be relatively stronger. This would be a dark-field motif where removing or adding background changes the contrast. The key difference from the interference hypothesis is that normalization predicts gain even with off-domain structured backgrounds, not just same-continent ones.

I need to set up a predictions matrix to distinguish these hypotheses. Then I'm moving to the measurement approach itself—the literal beats measurement using two operands superposed in a single slot, capturing residuals with each operand alone and together to test whether the superposed residual decomposes cleanly. So I need to distinguish between two registers: G1 captures the behavioral gain source (the causal discriminator between text and install conditions), while G2 measures the cross-term structure that reveals the mechanism. The experimental design fixes k at a gain-expressing value like 8 or 12 with k=1 as baseline, then compares a content-install arm that replicates the coherent-gain effect against a text-mention arm where real landmark names appear in the preamble without actual installs. Now I'm checking whether priming effects explain the gains—if A2 matches A1 while both exceed baseline, that suggests the benefit comes from semantic context rather than the underlying mechanism. I'll also examine how domain-specificity plays out by comparing A3 against A1, and whether the cross-term structure in G2 reveals genuine coherence or just linear medium effects versus noise. The key gate is replicating the gain at a chosen k value; without that expression, there's no valid verdict. The experimental cost stays manageable since k is fixed and I'm running roughly five arms across the draws.

For the validation phase, I'm querying only the target margin per draw per arm—just one forward pass needed. With 60 draws across 5 arms and 2 k-values, that's around 600 forwards plus capturing hidden states for cross-term pairs, maybe 40 pairs under 3 conditions, which should fit within 30–60 minutes on a 32B model. Then I'll test synthetic cases: a linear medium baseline with zero cross-terms, a nonlinear case where the structured cross-term gets recovered, and behavioral simulations comparing priming versus interference. The validation uses the paired permutation stats already in the DSP toolkit, and I'll plant simulated margins to verify whether gains come from installation effects or text-based improvements.

For the instrument, I'm extending the existing helper script to build a new cross-term detector that flags bilinear interactions while returning null for pure linear cases. I'll create a new script that reuses the margin-querying logic but adapts it for different arms, pulling from the existing model bank and directory structure. Since the current bank only covers geography, I need to add animal and product operands for the third condition—I can generate those using the same word-embedding machinery with a small custom list of animal and product terms. The question now is how much of the full pre-registration to draft in this pass. I'll draft the pre-reg section and add it to the convergence page below the Result-32B CAP section, keeping the stub in the candidates list with a pointer to the full section below. Before building the instrument, I need to present the key design decisions to Michael—particularly the G1 primary choice, the arms configuration, and the k parameter—since these are calls he typically weighs in on, and I want his approval or amendments before proceeding.

For the specifics: I'm recommending G1 primary as the source-of-gain discriminator with cross-terms as G2, a k_gain of 12 (balancing max gain without exhausting the bank), and including A3 off-domain since it's cheap and refines the design. I'm also flagging that 4B should be pre-registered as a no-gain host that will predictably fail the gate-0 contrast, so it functions as a null host for validating instrument mechanics rather than testing for actual gain.

I need to keep the pre-registration text uncommitted for now—draft it in the file and present the design calls without locking anything in yet.

Now I'm designing the holo_xterm instrument itself. The constants include an animal list for OFFDOM and K_GAIN/K_TREND parameters, and the --validate mode will simulate a verdict-table discriminator across three planted worlds (install-gain, priming, and contrast scenarios) to test the paired permutation gates.

For the model path, I'm loading landmarks with a ceiling check, building directories for both the geography list and the off-domain animals, then implementing a light verification that the animal operands have comparable embedding norms to the landmarks rather than a complex cloze-based check. Now I'm setting up the experimental arms across k values of 1, 6, and 12, where k=1 collapses to a single configuration. For each arm, I'm sampling a target landmark plus distractors and offdom words, then assigning nonces. A1 installs the target at the query slot with landmark distractors in the preamble. A2 lists real landmark names in the preamble text alongside the target nonce to test whether the model gets confused by mixing real names with a fake one. A3 places the target and offdom vectors at distractor slots in the preamble.

A4 uses random matched-norm vectors at distractor positions, and A5 is the bare condition with only the target installed. I'm running one forward pass per draw per arm, querying only the target. Then I'm comparing A1 versus A5 with a paired permutation test at k_gain to establish the gate—if that fails, the result is inconclusive. For the verdict, I'm computing Δ_install (A1−A2) and Δ_domain (A1−A3) as paired permutation tests, then classifying based on whether both deltas are significantly positive for interference-coherent, or if install isn't significant.

For G2, I'm sampling around 40 landmark pairs and capturing readout-slot residuals—I need to decide which layer to use. The pre-registration specifies readout-slot residuals, so I'll capture the last-token hidden states across all layers using output_hidden_states, then compute residuals for each pair.

The key insight is that for the cross-term probe, I should co-install both A and B at the same query slot using superposition—adding them together as a single vector—rather than spreading them across multiple slots. This gives me the single-slot HRR-trace variant, which is cleaner for cross-term algebra and distinct from G1's multi-slot geometry. I'll compute the difference X_l = r_AB(l) − r_A(l) − r_B(l) + r_0(l) per layer and measure the norm relative to the noise floor.

Now I'm setting up the analysis by using shuffled pairings as a null baseline to establish what the noise floor looks like, then projecting the difference signal onto three key axes: the sum direction (d_A + d_B), the difference direction (d_A − d_B), and the continent centroid axis, to see how much of the cross-term structure aligns with each.

For the implementation, I'm aggregating across all layers and reporting both per-layer curves and an overall profile, with default parameters of 10,000 permutations for the paired test. I also need to handle the preamble building for the holographic CAP analysis by passing real names and positioning the target nonce consistently at the end.

For the slot-finding logic, distractors in A1/A3/A4 correspond to nonce token positions, while A2 only has the target slot since it uses real names without installed nonces. I'll write out the full script now—around 450 lines—then validate with smoke tests on the 4B model before scaling to 32B, being careful with the paired permutation function to properly gate on the observed mean difference.

At the 4B gate check, I expect it to fail since there's no gain at that scale, so I'll print a clear warning but continue recording the arms anyway. The verdict scoring happens at the host level using the table regardless, with 32B designated as the verdict host.

--validate exercises it)\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\ndef g1_verdict(gate0_ok: bool, d_install, d_domain, a2_gain, a3_gain) -> str:\n    \"\"\"Frozen verdict table. d_* / *_gain are Gated objects (or None).\"\"\"\n    if not gate0_ok:\n        return \"negative/inconclusive (gate-0)\"\n    inst = d_install is not None and d_install.verdict\n    dom = d_domain is not None and d_domain.verdict\n    a2g = a2_gain is not None and a2_gain.verdict\n    a3g = a3_gain is not None and a3_gain.verdict\n    if inst and dom:\n        return \"INTERFERENCE-COHERENT\"\n    if (not inst) and a2g:\n        return \"PRIMING\"\n    if inst and (not dom) and a3g:\n        return \"CONTRAST-GENERIC\"\n    return \"MIXED\"\n\n\ndef xterm_stats(r_ab: np.ndarray, r_a: np.ndarray, r_b: np.ndarray,\n                r_0: np.ndarray, axes: dict[str, np.ndarray],\n                rng: np.random.Generator, n_shuf: int = 200) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"G2 at one layer over P pairs. r_*: (P, D). axes: name -> (P, D) unit.\n\n    X = r_ab - r_a - r_b + r_0. Magnitude vs shuffled-pair null (mismatched\n    A'/B' subtraction); structure = |cos(X, axis)| per declared axis vs the\n    same shuffled null.\n    \"\"\"\n    x = r_ab - r_a - r_b + r_0\n    xn = np.linalg.norm(x, axis=1)\n\n    def proj(mat):\n        out = {}\n        for name, ax in axes.items():\n            c = np.abs(np.sum(mat * ax, axis=1)\n                       / (np.linalg.norm(mat, axis=1)\n                          * np.linalg.norm(ax, axis=1) + 1e-12))\n            out[name] = float(np.mean(c))\n        return out\n\n    obs_proj = proj(x)\n    p = r_ab.shape[0]\n    null_norm, null_proj = [], {k: [] for k in axes}\n    for _ in range(n_shuf):\n        perm = rng.permutation(p)\n        # derangement-ish: avoid fixed points dominating small P\n        xs = r_ab - r_a[perm] - r_b[perm[::-1]] + r_0\n        null_norm.append(float(np.mean(np.linalg.norm(xs, axis=1))))\n        pr = proj(xs)\n        for k in axes:\n            null_proj[k].append(pr[k])\n    mean_xn = float(np.mean(xn))\n    nn = np.array(null_norm)\n    p_norm = float((1 + np.sum(nn >= mean_xn)) / (1 + nn.size))\n    struct = {}\n    for k in axes:\n        npk = np.array(null_proj[k])\n        struct[k] = {\"obs\": obs_proj[k], \"null_mean\": float(npk.mean()),\n                     \"p\": float((1 + np.sum(npk >= obs_proj[k]))\n                                / (1 + npk.size))}\n    return {\"mean_xnorm\": mean_xn, \"null_xnorm_mean\": float(nn.mean()),\n            \"p_norm_vs_shuffled\": p_norm, \"structure\": struct,\n            \"n_pairs\": int(p)}\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# --validate: planted worlds exercise verdict table + cross-term detector\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\ndef run_validate(alpha: float) -> int:\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)\n    print(\"── P-HOLO-XTERM --validate (planted worlds, no model) ──\")\n    ok = True\n    r, base, gain, noise = 60, 1.0, 1.3, 0.5\n\n    def world(a1, a2, a3):\n        m = {\"content\": base + a1 * gain + rng.normal(0, noise, r),\n             \"text\": base + a2 * gain + rng.normal(0, noise, r),\n             \"offdom\": base + a3 * gain + rng.normal(0, noise, r),\n             \"bare\": base + rng.normal(0, noise, r)}\n\n        def paired(x, y, name):\n            null = paired_permutation(m[x], m[y], rng)\n            return gate(float(np.mean(m[x] - m[y])), null, \"greater\",\n                        alpha, name=name)\n\n        g0 = paired(\"content\", \"bare\", \"gate0\")\n        return g1_verdict(g0.verdict, paired(\"content\", \"text\", \"d_install\"),\n                          paired(\"content\", \"offdom\", \"d_domain\"),\n                          paired(\"text\", \"bare\", \"a2_gain\"),\n                          paired(\"offdom\", \"bare\", \"a3_gain\"))\n\n    calls = {\"interference\": world(1, 0, 0), \"priming\": world(1, 1, 0),\n             \"contrast\": world(1, 0, 1)}\n    want = {\"interference\": \"INTERFERENCE-COHERENT\", \"priming\": \"PRIMING\",\n            \"contrast\": \"CONTRAST-GENERIC\"}\n    for w, call in calls.items():\n        good = call == want[w]\n        print(f\"[G1] {w}-world -> {call} (want {want[w]}) {'OK' if good else 'FAIL'}\")\n        ok &= good\n\n    # G2 detector: linear medium -> null; planted bilinear -> norm + structure\n    p_pairs, d = 40, 128\n    r_a = rng.standard_normal((p_pairs, d))\n    r_b = rng.standard_normal((p_pairs, d))\n    r_0 = rng.standard_normal((1, d)).repeat(p_pairs, axis=0)\n    axis = rng.standard_normal(d)\n    axis /= np.linalg.norm(axis)\n    axes = {\"planted\": np.tile(axis, (p_pairs, 1))}\n    lin = xterm_stats(r_a + r_b - r_0 + rng.normal(0, .05, (p_pairs, d)),\n                      r_a, r_b, r_0, axes, rng)\n    bil = xterm_stats(r_a + r_b - r_0 + 6.0 * axes[\"planted\"]\n                      + rng.normal(0, .05, (p_pairs, d)),\n                      r_a, r_b, r_0, axes, rng)\n    lin_ok = bool(lin[\"p_norm_vs_shuffled\"] > alpha)\n    bil_ok = bool(bil[\"structure\"][\"planted\"][\"p\"] < alpha\n                  and bil[\"structure\"][\"planted\"][\"obs\"]\n                  > 3 * bil[\"structure\"][\"planted\"][\"null_mean\"])\n    print(f\"[G2] linear plant: p_norm={lin['p_norm_vs_shuffled']:.3f} \"\n          f\"(want >{alpha}) {'OK' if lin_ok else 'FAIL'}\")\n    print(f\"[G2] bilinear plant: proj={bil['structure']['planted']['obs']:.3f} \"\n          f\"vs null {bil['structure']['planted']['null_mean']:.3f} \"\n          f\"p={bil['structure']['planted']['p']:.3f} {'OK' if bil_ok else 'FAIL'}\")\n    ok &= lin_ok and bil_ok\n    print(f\"\\n── --validate {'ALL PASS' if ok else 'FAIL'} ──\")\n    return 0 if ok else 1\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Model path\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\ndef run_model(args) -> int:\n    import operand_multihop3 as mh3\n    import torch\n    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\n\n    dev = (args.device if (args.device != \"mps\" or torch.backends.mps.is_available())\n           else \"cpu\")\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(args.seed)\n    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(args.model_id)\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\n        args.model_id, dtype=getattr(torch, args.dtype)).to(dev).eval()\n    dec, _n, _u = mh3.resolve_parts(model)\n    L, S = args.ref_layer, args.scale\n    print(f\"[xterm] {args.model_id} L_ref={L} scale={S} dev={dev} \"\n          f\"n_layers={len(dec)}\")\n\n    cont_ids = {c: mh3.first_tid(tok, c) for c in mh3.CONTINENTS}\n    nonce_tid, nonces = {}, []\n    for n in NONCE_CANDS:\n        t = tok(\" \" + n, add_special_tokens=False).input_ids[-1]\n        if t not in nonce_tid.values():\n            nonce_tid[n] = t\n            nonces.append(n)\n\n    # ceiling (holo_cap pattern)\n    def real_pred(prefix, query, word, label_ids):\n        ids = tok(prefix + query.format(x=word), return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()\n        return max(label_ids, key=lambda k: lo[label_ids[k]])\n\n    city_ids = {c: mh3.first_tid(tok, c) for c in mh3.CITIES}\n    country_ids = {c: mh3.first_tid(tok, c) for c in mh3.COUNTRIES}\n    valid = []\n    for lm in mh3.LM_LIST:\n        ok = (real_pred(mh3.CITY_PREFIX, mh3.CITY_QUERY, lm, city_ids)\n              == mh3.CITY_OF[lm]\n              and real_pred(mh3.CITY2COUNTRY_PREFIX, mh3.CITY2COUNTRY_QUERY,\n                            mh3.CITY_OF[lm], country_ids)\n              == mh3.CITY_COUNTRY[mh3.CITY_OF[lm]]\n              and real_pred(mh3.COUNTRY2CONT_PREFIX, mh3.COUNTRY2CONT_QUERY,\n                            mh3.COUNTRY_OF[lm], cont_ids)\n              == mh3.COUNTRY_CONT[mh3.COUNTRY_OF[lm]])\n        if ok:\n            valid.append(lm)\n    print(f\"[xterm] valid landmarks: {len(valid)}/{len(mh3.LM_LIST)}\")\n\n    def build_dirs(items):\n        per = {e: [] for e in items}\n        for fr in mh3.FRAMES:\n            for e in items:\n                store: dict[int, np.ndarray] = {}\n                h = dec[L].register_forward_hook(mh3.cap_hook(store, L))\n                ids = tok(fr.format(x=e), return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n                with torch.no_grad():\n                    model(**ids)\n                h.remove()\n                per[e].append(store[L][0, -2, :])\n        em = {e: np.mean(per[e], axis=0) for e in items}\n        gm = np.mean([em[e] for e in items], axis=0)\n        return {e: em[e] - gm for e in items}\n\n    d_lm = build_dirs(mh3.LM_LIST)\n    d_off = build_dirs(OFFDOM)\n    dim = d_lm[valid[0]].shape[0]\n    lm_norm = float(np.mean([np.linalg.norm(d_lm[x]) for x in valid]))\n    off_norm = float(np.mean([np.linalg.norm(d_off[x]) for x in OFFDOM]))\n    # norm-match offdomain operands to the landmark operand scale (recorded)\n    off_scale = lm_norm / (off_norm + 1e-9)\n    print(f\"[xterm] operand norms: lm={lm_norm:.2f} offdom={off_norm:.2f} \"\n          f\"(offdom matched x{off_scale:.2f})\")\n\n    def rand_vec(norm):\n        v = rng.standard_normal(dim)\n        return v / (np.linalg.norm(v) + 1e-9) * norm\n\n    def forward_margin(prompt, adds):\n        ids = tok(prompt, return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        toks = ids.input_ids[0].tolist()\n        handles = []\n        for vec, tid, which in adds:\n            occ = [i for i, t in enumerate(toks) if t == tid]\n            if not occ:\n                continue\n            pos = occ[-1] if which == \"last\" else occ[0]\n            vt = torch.tensor(vec, dtype=torch.float32, device=dev)\n            handles.append(dec[L].register_forward_hook(\n                mh3.add_hook_at(vt, pos)))\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()\n        for hd in handles:\n            hd.remove()\n        return lo\n\n    def margin_of(lo, lm):\n        truth = mh3.CONT_OF[lm]\n        others = [c for c in mh3.CONTINENTS if c != truth]\n        return float(lo[cont_ids[truth]]\n                     - max(lo[cont_ids[c]] for c in others))\n\n    def arm_margin(arm, k, tgt, d_lms, d_offs):\n        \"\"\"One draw, one arm, query the target. k-1 distractors.\"\"\"\n        tgt_nonce = nonces[0]\n        d_nonces = nonces[1:k]\n        if arm == \"text\":\n            names = [f\"the {x}\" for x in d_lms] + [f\"the {tgt_nonce}\"]\n            order = rng.permutation(len(names))\n            items = [names[i][4:] for i in order]  # strip \"the \" for builder\n            pre = build_preamble(items)\n            adds = [(d_lm[tgt] * S, nonce_tid[tgt_nonce], \"last\")]\n        else:\n            pre = build_preamble([tgt_nonce, *d_nonces])\n            adds = [(d_lm[tgt] * S, nonce_tid[tgt_nonce], \"last\")]\n            for i, dn in enumerate(d_nonces):\n                if arm == \"content\":\n                    vec = d_lm[d_lms[i]] * S\n                elif arm == \"offdom\":\n                    vec = d_off[d_offs[i]] * off_scale * S\n                elif arm == \"random\":\n                    vec = rand_vec(np.linalg.norm(d_lm[d_lms[i]] * S))\n                else:  # bare\n                    continue\n                adds.append((vec, nonce_tid[dn], \"first\"))\n        prompt = mh3.CONT_PREFIX + pre + mh3.CONT_QUERY.format(x=tgt_nonce)\n        return margin_of(forward_margin(prompt, adds), tgt)\n\n    # ── G1/G3: arms x k-trend, paired draws ────────────────────────────────\n    k_trend = [k for k in args.k_trend if k <= min(len(valid), len(nonces))]\n    k_gain = min(args.k_gain, max(k_trend))\n    arms_data = {a: {str(k): [] for k in k_trend} for a in ARMS}\n    for k in k_trend:\n        for _ in range(args.draws):\n            tgt = str(rng.choice(valid))\n            pool = [x for x in valid if x != tgt]\n            d_lms = [str(x) for x in rng.choice(pool, k - 1, replace=False)] \\\n                if k > 1 else []\n            d_offs = [str(x) for x in rng.choice(OFFDOM, k - 1, replace=False)] \\\n                if k > 1 else []\n            for a in ARMS:\n                if k == 1 and a != \"content\":\n                    continue  # k=1: all arms identical; content stands in\n                arms_data[a][str(k)].append(arm_margin(a, k, tgt, d_lms, d_offs))\n        row = {a: (round(float(np.mean(arms_data[a][str(k)])), 3)\n                   if arms_data[a][str(k)] else None) for a in ARMS}\n        print(f\"[xterm] k={k}: {row}\")\n\n    def arr(a, k):\n        return np.asarray(arms_data[a][str(k)], dtype=float)\n\n    def paired(x, y, name):\n        a, b = arr(x, k_gain), arr(y, k_gain)\n        null = paired_permutation(a, b, rng)\n        return gate(float(np.mean(a - b)), null, \"greater\", args.alpha,\n                    name=name)\n\n    g0 = paired(\"content\", \"bare\", \"gate0_gain\")\n    d_install = paired(\"content\", \"text\", \"delta_install\")\n    d_domain = paired(\"content\", \"offdom\", \"delta_domain\")\n    a2_gain = paired(\"text\", \"bare\", \"a2_gain\")\n    a3_gain = paired(\"offdom\", \"bare\", \"a3_gain\")\n    verdict = g1_verdict(g0.verdict, d_install, d_domain, a2_gain, a3_gain)\n    print(f\"[xterm] gate-0 gain={g0.value:.3f} p={g0.p:.4f} \"\n          f\"expressed={g0.verdict}\")\n    print(f\"[xterm] G1: d_install={d_install.value:.3f} (p={d_install.p:.4f}) \"\n          f\"d_domain={d_domain.value:.3f} (p={d_domain.p:.4f}) | \"\n          f\"a2_gain p={a2_gain.p:.4f} a3_gain p={a3_gain.p:.4f}\")\n    print(f\"[xterm] G1 VERDICT -> {verdict}\")\n\n    # ── G2: single-slot cross-terms over pairs ─────────────────────────────\n    def capture_resid(adds_vec):\n        \"\"\"Last-token residuals at all layers; single query-slot installs.\"\"\"\n        prompt = mh3.CONT_PREFIX + build_preamble([nonces[0]]) \\\n            + mh3.CONT_QUERY.format(x=nonces[0])\n        ids = tok(prompt, return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        toks = ids.input_ids[0].tolist()\n        occ = [i for i, t in enumerate(toks) if t == nonce_tid[nonces[0]]]\n        pos = occ[-1]\n        handles = []\n        if adds_vec is not None:\n            vt = torch.tensor(adds_vec, dtype=torch.float32, device=dev)\n            handles.append(dec[L].register_forward_hook(\n                mh3.add_hook_at(vt, pos)))\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            out = model(**ids, output_hidden_states=True)\n        for hd in handles:\n            hd.remove()\n        return np.stack([h[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()\n                         for h in out.hidden_states])  # (n_layers+1, D)\n\n    pairs = []\n    pool = list(valid)\n    for _ in range(args.n_pairs):\n        a, b = (str(x) for x in rng.choice(pool, 2, replace=False))\n        pairs.append((a, b))\n    r0 = capture_resid(None)\n    singles = {}\n    for lm in {x for p in pairs for x in p}:\n        singles[lm] = capture_resid(d_lm[lm] * S)\n    n_lay = r0.shape[0]\n    lay_sel = list(range(2, n_lay - 1))\n    g2_layers = {}\n    r_ab_all, r_a_all, r_b_all, ax_sum, ax_diff, ax_cont = [], [], [], [], [], []\n    cont_axis = {c: np.mean([d_lm[x] for x in valid\n                             if mh3.CONT_OF[x] == c], axis=0)\n                 for c in mh3.CONTINENTS}\n    for (a, b) in pairs:\n        r_ab_all.append(capture_resid((d_lm[a] + d_lm[b]) * S))\n        r_a_all.append(singles[a])\n        r_b_all.append(singles[b])\n        s_ax = d_lm[a] + d_lm[b]\n        d_ax = d_lm[a] - d_lm[b]\n        c_ax = cont_axis[mh3.CONT_OF[a]] + cont_axis[mh3.CONT_OF[b]]\n        ax_sum.append(s_ax / (np.linalg.norm(s_ax) + 1e-12))\n        ax_diff.append(d_ax / (np.linalg.norm(d_ax) + 1e-12))\n        ax_cont.append(c_ax / (np.linalg.norm(c_ax) + 1e-12))\n    ax_sum, ax_diff, ax_cont = map(np.stack, (ax_sum, ax_diff, ax_cont))\n    for li in lay_sel:\n        g2_layers[str(li)] = xterm_stats(\n            np.stack([r[li] for r in r_ab_all]),\n            np.stack([r[li] for r in r_a_all]),\n            np.stack([r[li] for r in r_b_all]),\n            np.tile(r0[li], (len(pairs), 1)),\n            {\"sum\": ax_sum, \"diff\": ax_diff, \"continent\": ax_cont}, rng)\n    # aggregate: median across layers\n    agg = {\"p_norm_median\": float(np.median(\n        [g2_layers[k][\"p_norm_vs_shuffled\"] for k in g2_layers]))}\n    for axn in (\"sum\", \"diff\", \"continent\"):\n        agg[f\"{axn}_p_median\"] = float(np.median(\n            [g2_layers[k][\"structure\"][axn][\"p\"] for k in g2_layers]))\n        agg[f\"{axn}_obs_median\"] = float(np.median(\n            [g2_layers[k][\"structure\"][axn][\"obs\"] for k in g2_layers]))\n    print(f\"[xterm] G2 aggregate: {agg}\")\n\n    result = {\n        \"model_id\": args.model_id, \"seed\": args.seed, \"scale\": S,\n        \"ref_layer\": L, \"k_trend\": k_trend, \"k_gain\": k_gain,\n        \"draws\": args.draws, \"alpha\": args.alpha, \"n_pairs\": len(pairs),\n        \"valid_landmarks\": valid, \"offdom\": OFFDOM,\n        \"operand_norms\": {\"lm\": lm_norm, \"offdom_raw\": off_norm,\n                          \"offdom_scale\": off_scale},\n        \"arms_data\": {a: arms_data[a] for a in ARMS},\n        \"g1\": {\"gate0\": asdict(g0), \"delta_install\": asdict(d_install),\n               \"delta_domain\": asdict(d_domain), \"a2_gain\": asdict(a2_gain),\n               \"a3_gain\": asdict(a3_gain), \"verdict\": verdict},\n        \"g2\": {\"per_layer\": g2_layers, \"aggregate\": agg},\n    }\n    out = Path(args.out)\n    out.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    (out / \"holo_xterm.json\").write_text(\n        json.dumps(_json_safe(result), indent=2, allow_nan=False))\n    print(f\"[xterm] wrote {out}/holo_xterm.json\")\n    return 0\n\n\ndef main() -> int:\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=\"P-HOLO-XTERM interference terms\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--validate\", action=\"store_true\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--model-id\", default=\"Qwen/Qwen3-4B\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--device\", default=\"mps\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--dtype\", default=\"bfloat16\", choices=[\"float32\", \"bfloat16\"])\n    ap.add_argument(\"--ref-layer\", type=int, default=9)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--scale\", type=float, default=2.0)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--draws\", type=int, default=12)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--k-trend\", type=int, nargs=\"+\",\n                    default=list(K_TREND_DEFAULT))\n    ap.add_argument(\"--k-gain\", type=int, default=K_GAIN_DEFAULT)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--n-pairs\", type=int, default=40)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--alpha\", type=float, default=0.05)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--seed\", type=int, default=0)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--out\", default=\"results/holo-xterm/qwen3-4b\")\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n    if args.validate:\n        return run_validate(args.alpha)\n    return run_model(args)\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    raise SystemExit(main())\n"
}

### Thinking

Looking at the 4B smoke test results, I'm noticing something unexpected — Gate-0 shows a gain of 0.76 (p=0.007) at 4B, which contradicts our pre-registration that marked 4B as a no-gain host based on the CAP curve. But examining the data more closely, there actually was a content-versus-bare gap in CAP at 4B too, so this isn't entirely inconsistent with what we saw before.

The key insight is that my "no gain" label was sloppy — I was referring to the k-trend (the curve staying flat rather than rising), not the absolute gain-versus-floor difference. At 4B, the gain exists but doesn't scale with k. The smoke test shows text arm (2.479) and random (2.562) both track with content (2.24) at k=12, which mirrors the CAP pattern where random was similarly high. This suggests the 4B gain is driven by energy and priming effects rather than content-specific interference — everything with activation produces the same boost. For G2 at 4B, the cross-terms show no structure (p_norm 1.0), indicating a linear medium for single-slot pairs, with the diff axis barely significant at p=0.13.

The critical distinction emerges at 32B, where CAP showed random does not reproduce the content effect.

Now I need to reconcile the pre-registration claim that 4B should show no gain with what I actually observed — the gate-0 expression does appear at 4B, so I'll note this honestly in the results. The 32B run is where the real discrimination happens. I'm committing the code and results, then launching the 32B experiment in the main pipeline. The computational cost breaks down to roughly 660 forwards for G1 (40–50 minutes) and a few minutes for G2's 40 pairs with hidden states, totaling around an hour with 60 draws and 40 pairs.

### Thinking

I found that at 4B the gain exists but is content-nonspecific across text, random, and content conditions, suggesting advisory priming rather than content-dependent effects—the real discrimination appears at 32B where random fails to reproduce the gain. I'm committing to this verdict now.

### Thinking

Looking at the XTERM 32B results from the frozen table analysis, the verdict is interference-coherent. The gate-0 shows a significant gain of 2.158 with strong statistical significance, and the installation delta indicates that content exposure in the medium itself drives the effect rather than just semantic meaning. The domain delta further confirms that in-domain coherence matters more than generic structural coherence.

Examining the performance ladder across conditions at k=12, content substantially outperforms text, which outperforms off-domain, which outperforms random, which outperforms bare — a clean monotonic ordering that decomposes the gain into components: priming contributes roughly 50% of the effect, generic structure about 30%, and the remaining 20% comes from medium-specific content. The cross-term analysis for G2 shows no significant structure along the sum or continent axes, indicating the medium operates linearly at this grain.

The key insight is that behavioral interference doesn't manifest as nonlinearity in single-slot vector addition — instead, the gain emerges in multi-slot geometry through attention-mediated retrieval. The residual space superposes linearly as expected, but the actual interference happens at the retrieval and attention step across multiple slots. This maps cleanly to optical holography: the recording medium is linear, but interference occurs during reconstruction under illumination. So the plate stores information linearly while the beam does the interfering work.

Looking at the 4B versus 32B scale comparison, there's a striking pattern where priming dominates at 4B (content essentially random) but interference-coherence takes over at 32B — this is the fourth occurrence of that qualitative flip between these scales. The runtime measurement is coming in around six and a half minutes total.

## Assistant

Six minutes of compute, and the frozen table speaks: **INTERFERENCE-COHERENT** — with a mechanism twist from G2 that makes it the most on-thesis outcome available. Scoring:

**G1 (frozen gates, all p ≤ 0.0004):** the k=12 arms form a perfect monotone ladder —

```
content 2.84 > text 2.01 > offdom 1.62 > random 0.92 > bare 0.68
```

- gate-0: gain +2.16, p=0.0001 ✓
- **Δ_install = +0.83, p=0.0004** — meaning alone does NOT reproduce the gain; the exposures must be in the medium
- **Δ_domain = +1.21, p=0.0001** — any-structured-background does NOT reproduce it; in-domain coherence required
- But a2/a3 gains are also real (both p=0.0001): the ladder *decomposes* the 2.2 gain into ~1.3 priming + ~0.9 structure + ~0.8 medium-specific — priming exists, it's just not the whole story. The verdict clause fires on both deltas: **INTERFERENCE-COHERENT.**

**G2 (the register twist):** cross-terms are **dead linear** — r(A⊕B) ≈ r(A)+r(B)−r(0), no magnitude excess (p_norm=1.0), no structure on any declared axis. The plate records *linearly*; single-slot superposition doesn't mix. So the interference measured in G1 is not stored nonlinearity — it's enacted at **retrieval, across slots, in the attention step**. Which is precisely optical holography: *the recording medium is linear; interference happens in the light.* The plate doesn't interfere — the beam does.

**4B→32B**: PRIMING at 4B → INTERFERENCE-COHERENT at 32B (the qualitative scale-flip motif, ~4th occurrence) — scale grows the medium-specific component from zero.

## Assistant

Committed (2066101). The brief:

**The question:** CAP found that packing more coherent memories into the context made retrieval *better*, not worse. Why? Three suspects: the meaning of the extra content (priming), the mere presence of structure (contrast), or genuine interaction between the stored vectors in the medium (interference).

**The test:** same setup, five arms that differ in exactly one thing. Real installed operands vs the same landmarks *named in plain text* vs coherent *animal* operands vs random vectors vs nothing.

**The result:** a clean staircase — content 2.84 > text 2.01 > animals 1.62 > random 0.92 > empty 0.68. Text priming helps (that part's real), any structure helps a bit, but the biggest and only *compounding* piece requires **real, in-domain vectors physically installed in the medium**. That's interference, not psychology. Verdict: INTERFERENCE-COHERENT.

**The twist:** when we checked the storage directly — install two operands in one slot and look for nonlinear mixing — there was *none*. Perfectly linear: A+B stores as exactly A plus B.

**So:** the stored vectors don't interact where they *sit*; they interact when they're *read* — in the attention step that sweeps across them. Which is literally how optical holograms work: the photographic plate is a dumb linear recording, and all the interference happens in the light passing through it. The model's plate is linear; **attention is the light** — and that's where the physics of this machine lives.

Bonus: small model shows only the priming part; the interference component *emerges with scale*.

The theory frames it as index(f) = ⟨key(f), window(f), product(f)⟩, where the key is the content direction that dispatches function selection. Currently, function selection happens through query text (like a cloze asking "is located in the city of __"), but P-FN-INDEX aims to flip this: can we select functions through injected content instead? I'm exploring whether we can construct function-key vectors by computing the difference-of-means of residuals across prompts that invoke specific maps versus others—essentially extracting task vectors from attention patterns that, when injected, trigger execution of the desired function.

For the concrete design, I'm setting up two domains: geography maps (city-of, country-of, continent-of) operating on landmarks, and animals maps (class-of for mammal/bird/fish, covering-of for fur/feathers/scales) operating on simple animals. The key construction extracts the function vector as the mean residual at a mid-layer across prompts that exercise each map.

For the dispatch test, I'm presenting an ambiguous query that doesn't name the map but has the operand installed, injecting the map's key at a seam layer to see which map's output emerges. The readout compares predicted answers against closed candidate sets for each domain's product space, building a dispatch matrix to measure whether the injected key steers toward the correct map versus random performance.

On the prompt design, I'm moving toward a zero-shot approach inspired by function vectors: a neutral template like "Q: the {nonce}. A:" where the key gets injected at specific layers, and I grade by checking which map's first token wins the argmax over all candidates. The key question is whether to inject at the final token position (the "A:" slot) or across a window in the query itself, and I'm setting up gates to validate that operands resolve correctly under explicit text queries.

For the main dispatch test, I'm building a matrix across five maps to measure whether injecting the right key for a function produces answers in that function's output space. The verdict hinges on three comparisons: diagonal accuracy should beat shuffled keys (wrong function), beat the no-key baseline, and beat random vectors—all with p<0.05. I need to verify this holds both within-domain (same operand across geography maps like city vs country) and across families (geography keys shouldn't activate animal keys).

The secondary checks are whether key vectors cluster in low-rank subspaces and where in the network depth the injection works best.

For validation, I'm testing against baselines: shuffled keys, random vectors, and no-key controls, plus a synthetic setup where planted key directions should rotate the output into the map's product space—diagonal dispatch matrix for real keys, flat for noise. I'm building the test dataset with eighteen balanced animals across three classes: mammals, birds, and fish. layer for injection, matching the approach in function-vector papers. I'm deciding whether to pre-register a fixed relative-depth window like 40-70% of the model's layers or just inject at a single middle layer determined by preliminary 4B experiments, then apply that same choice to the 32B model.

I'm settling on a compromise: sweep across five candidate relative depths (0.3, 0.4, 0.5, 0.6, 0.7) to find the best injection layer, but apply multiple-comparison correction by computing the null distribution at that same best layer so the null gets the same selection advantage. For the prompt format, I need something minimal that elicits a one-word answer—something like "{nonce}:" following the function-vector paper style.

Now I'm refining the grading approach: instead of requiring natural language generation, I'll just compare logits across a closed set of candidate products (all combinations across the maps, roughly 12 cities worth). The score is the argmax of first-token IDs from all candidates, and it's correct if that matches the first token of the ground truth. The margin is the difference between the logit for the true answer and the highest logit among other candidates—this sidesteps the vagueness problem since I only need relative logit rankings. For a 32B model, I'm estimating around 40 forward passes (5 maps × ~8 exemplar prompts) plus 18 cells to evaluate.

The full experiment involves 18 landmarks × 3 geographic keys, 18 animals × 2 animal keys, plus cross-category combinations, totaling roughly 180 cells across 5 layers with 7 different conditions each (key, shuffled variants, random, no-key)—that's about 6,300 forward passes, taking 40–60 minutes on a 32B model, which is manageable overnight. I can trim the null cases from the shuffled-key cells themselves. This is buildable within the turn budget, though it's a lot of work: setting up the bank, pre-registration, the instrument (~500 lines), validation, smoke tests, and launch. I need to be efficient about it.

For the pre-registration section, I'll keep it compact on the program-plates page. I also need to handle the animal operand installation using generic frames, validate that animals map correctly to both their class and covering via few-shot prefixes, and only keep animals where both links hold. When injecting the key vector, I'll scale it to match the typical residual norm—using a separate `--key-scale` parameter defaulting to 2.0, since keys represent residual-scale means. The baseline concern is what happens with a neutral prompt and the installed operand but no key—it'll probably default to continent or city associations, and that becomes the no-key baseline row that the keyed version needs to beat.

For correctness, I'm measuring margin per cell as the logit of the first token from f(X) minus the max over all other options, with accuracy defined as margin > 0. The G1 statistic compares the mean diagonal margin (key_f → margin_f) against a null distribution from shuffled-key margins (key_g → margin_f where g ≠ f) using a paired permutation test. I also need to check cross-domain behavior separately—geo keys on animal operands shouldn't produce geo products above baseline, and I'll restrict the dispatch matrix to only valid operand-map pairs: geo operands paired with geo maps, animal operands with animal maps.

For validation, I'm using a synthetic linear toy model with dimension 64 where the operand vector acts as a residual, and I'm simulating margins directly: diagonal cells drawn from N(1.5, σ), off-diagonal from N(0, σ), and random-key from N(0, σ) to test whether the verdict logic correctly identifies the flat world case. I'll keep the validation focused on the statistics themselves.

Now I'm writing the pre-registration into the program-plates page as a tight section, and I'm placing the animal bank module constants directly into scripts/explore/fn_index.py so the census can import from it later. For key extraction, I'm using the existing few-shot prefix lines format to generate exemplar prompts for each map. Settling on using just the three held-out exemplars per map to build keys—extracting residuals from the final token position before the answer across a few prompt variants, then averaging those three and subtracting the grand mean across all maps to isolate the map-specific state.

For the animal map, I'm defining two attribute templates: one for classification (mammal/bird/fish) and another for body covering (fur/feathers/scales), using held-out exemplars like wolf, parrot, and carp to avoid overlap with the main bank of eighteen animals across those three classes.

Now I'm building the union candidate set for grading by combining cities, countries, continents, and the animal classifications with their coverings, deduplicating any collisions in the first tokens. I'm using a neutral prompt structure that pushes toward noun continuations, and I'll inject the operand at the standard reference position.

For the actual evaluation, I'm sweeping across four different layer depths to find where the model best matches the selection, then running forward passes across 90 cells (54 geographic, 36 animal) with seven different conditioning configurations per cell per layer — that's about 2,520 forward passes total, plus additional passes to capture hidden states and build the embedding frames.

The full 32B model run will take roughly 20-25 minutes overnight, but I can smoke-test with 4B using fewer cells to validate the approach. For statistics, I'm computing diagonal margins per layer against shuffled-key baselines, then using paired permutation tests to identify the best layer and report significance with Bonferroni correction across the four layers.

The verdict hinges on whether the diagonal beats both shuffled-key and random baselines at α/4 threshold, with the additional check that diagonal mean accuracy exceeds the no-key baseline. I'm also distinguishing between indexed dispatch (where other-domain keys fail the union null) versus partial within-domain effects (where same-domain contrasts pass but cross-domain keys still activate).

Now I'm moving into implementation: starting with the pre-registration section, freezing the commit, writing the instrument, running validation and smoke tests, then launching and verifying results before final commit. I'll keep the code compact given the scope.

(dropped collisions: \"\n          f\"{sorted(drop)})\")\n\n    # ── ceilings ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────\n    def real_pred(prefix, query, word, labels):\n        ids = tok(prefix + query.format(x=word), return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()\n        return max(labels, key=lambda k: lo[first_tid(k)])\n\n    valid_geo = []\n    for lm in mh3.LM_LIST:\n        if (real_pred(mh3.CITY_PREFIX, mh3.CITY_QUERY, lm, mh3.CITIES)\n                == mh3.CITY_OF[lm]\n                and real_pred(mh3.COUNTRY_PREFIX, mh3.COUNTRY_QUERY, lm,\n                              mh3.COUNTRIES) == mh3.COUNTRY_OF[lm]\n                and real_pred(mh3.CONT_PREFIX, mh3.CONT_QUERY, lm,\n                              mh3.CONTINENTS) == mh3.CONT_OF[lm]):\n            valid_geo.append(lm)\n    valid_an = []\n    for a in AN_LIST:\n        if (real_pred(CLASS_PREFIX, CLASS_QUERY, a, CLASSES) == CLASS_OF[a]\n                and real_pred(COVER_PREFIX, COVER_QUERY, a, COVERS)\n                == COVER_OF[a]):\n            valid_an.append(a)\n    print(f\"[fni] ceilings: geo {len(valid_geo)}/18 animal {len(valid_an)}/18\")\n    gate0 = bool(len(valid_geo) >= 10 and len(valid_an) >= 10)\n    if args.n_per_domain:\n        valid_geo = valid_geo[:args.n_per_domain]\n        valid_an = valid_an[:args.n_per_domain]\n\n    # ── operand directions (both domains, one pooled build per domain) ─────\n    def build_dirs(items):\n        per = {e: [] for e in items}\n        for fr in mh3.FRAMES:\n            for e in items:\n                store: dict[int, np.ndarray] = {}\n                h = dec[L].register_forward_hook(mh3.cap_hook(store, L))\n                ids = tok(fr.format(x=e), return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n                with torch.no_grad():\n                    model(**ids)\n                h.remove()\n                per[e].append(store[L][0, -2, :])\n        em = {e: np.mean(per[e], axis=0) for e in items}\n        gm = np.mean([em[e] for e in items], axis=0)\n        return {e: em[e] - gm for e in items}\n\n    d_geo = build_dirs(mh3.LM_LIST)\n    d_an = build_dirs(AN_LIST)\n    d_of = {**{(\"geo\", x): d_geo[x] for x in mh3.LM_LIST},\n            **{(\"animal\", x): d_an[x] for x in AN_LIST}}\n    dim = d_geo[mh3.LM_LIST[0]].shape[0]\n\n    # ── keys: held-out exemplars, last-token residual per inj layer ────────\n    def capture_last(prompt):\n        ids = tok(prompt, return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            out = model(**ids, output_hidden_states=True)\n        return {li: out.hidden_states[li + 1][0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()\n                for li in inj_layers}\n\n    raw_keys = {m: {li: [] for li in inj_layers} for m in ALL_MAPS}\n    for m in ALL_MAPS:\n        for word, tpl in KEY_EXEMPLARS[m]:\n            caps = capture_last(tpl.format(x=word))\n            for li in inj_layers:\n                raw_keys[m][li].append(caps[li])\n    keys = {}\n    for li in inj_layers:\n        means = {m: np.mean(raw_keys[m][li], axis=0) for m in ALL_MAPS}\n        gm = np.mean(list(means.values()), axis=0)\n        for m in ALL_MAPS:\n            keys[(m, li)] = means[m] - gm\n    key_norms = {m: float(np.linalg.norm(keys[(m, inj_layers[0])]))\n                 for m in ALL_MAPS}\n    print(f\"[fni] key norms @L{inj_layers[0]}: \"\n          f\"{ {m: round(v, 1) for m, v in key_norms.items()} }\")\n\n    def rand_like(vec):\n        v = rng.standard_normal(dim)\n        return v / (np.linalg.norm(v) + 1e-9) * float(np.linalg.norm(vec))\n\n    # ── one dispatch forward ───────────────────────────────────────────────\n    def cell_logits(domain, item, key_vec, li):\n        prompt = NEUTRAL.format(x=nonce)\n        ids = tok(prompt, return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        toks = ids.input_ids[0].tolist()\n        occ = [i for i, t in enumerate(toks) if t == nonce_tid]\n        handles = []\n        vt = torch.tensor(d_of[(domain, item)] * S, dtype=torch.float32,\n                          device=dev)\n        handles.append(dec[L].register_forward_hook(\n            mh3.add_hook_at(vt, occ[-1])))\n        if key_vec is not None:\n            kt = torch.tensor(key_vec * args.key_scale, dtype=torch.float32,\n                              device=dev)\n            handles.append(dec[li].register_forward_hook(\n                mh3.add_hook_at(kt, len(toks) - 1)))\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()\n        for hd in handles:\n            hd.remove()\n        return lo\n\n    def margin_and_hit(lo, m, item):\n        prod = product_of[(m, item)]\n        if prod not in union:\n            return None, None\n        others = [t for w, t in union.items() if w != prod]\n        marg = float(lo[union[prod]] - max(lo[t] for t in others))\n        return marg, bool(marg > 0)\n\n    cells = ([(\"geo\", lm, m) for lm in valid_geo for m in GEO_MAPS]\n             + [(\"animal\", a, m) for a in valid_an for m in AN_MAPS])\n    cells = [(d, i, m) for (d, i, m) in cells\n             if product_of[(m, i)] in union]\n    print(f\"[fni] cells: {len(cells)}\")\n\n    per_layer = {}\n    records = []\n    for li in inj_layers:\n        diag, within, uni, rand_m, nokey_m = [], [], [], [], []\n        diag_hit, nokey_hit = [], []\n        for (dom, item, m) in cells:\n            lo_d = cell_logits(dom, item, keys[(m, li)], li)\n            mg_d, hit_d = margin_and_hit(lo_d, m, item)\n            others_w, others_u = [], []\n            for m2 in ALL_MAPS:\n                if m2 == m:\n                    continue\n                lo_o = cell_logits(dom, item, keys[(m2, li)], li)\n                mg_o, _ = margin_and_hit(lo_o, m, item)\n                others_u.append(mg_o)\n                if DOMAIN_OF[m2] == DOMAIN_OF[m]:\n                    others_w.append(mg_o)\n            lo_r = cell_logits(dom, item, rand_like(keys[(m, li)]), li)\n            mg_r, _ = margin_and_hit(lo_r, m, item)\n            lo_0 = cell_logits(dom, item, None, li)\n            mg_0, hit_0 = margin_and_hit(lo_0, m, item)\n            diag.append(mg_d)\n            within.append(float(np.mean(others_w)) if others_w else mg_d)\n            uni.append(float(np.mean(others_u)))\n            rand_m.append(mg_r)\n            nokey_m.append(mg_0)\n            diag_hit.append(hit_d)\n            nokey_hit.append(hit_0)\n            records.append({\"layer\": li, \"domain\": dom, \"item\": item,\n                            \"map\": m, \"diag\": mg_d,\n                            \"within_null\": within[-1], \"union_null\": uni[-1],\n                            \"random\": mg_r, \"nokey\": mg_0})\n        diag = np.asarray(diag)\n        sc = score_layer(diag, np.asarray(within), np.asarray(uni), rng,\n                         alpha_sel)\n        g_rand = gate(float(np.mean(diag - np.asarray(rand_m))),\n                      paired_permutation(diag, np.asarray(rand_m), rng),\n                      \"greater\", alpha_sel, name=\"vs_random\")\n        acc_d = float(np.mean(diag_hit))\n        acc_0 = float(np.mean(nokey_hit))\n        per_layer[str(li)] = {\n            \"score\": {k: (asdict(v) if hasattr(v, \"p\") else v)\n                      for k, v in sc.items()},\n            \"vs_random\": asdict(g_rand), \"diag_acc\": acc_d,\n            \"nokey_acc\": acc_0,\n            \"_g\": (sc, g_rand, acc_d, acc_0)}\n        print(f\"[fni] L{li}: diag={sc['diag_mean']:.3f} \"\n              f\"d_within={sc['contrast_within'].value:+.3f} \"\n              f\"(p={sc['contrast_within'].p:.4f}) \"\n              f\"d_union={sc['contrast_union'].value:+.3f} \"\n              f\"(p={sc['contrast_union'].p:.4f}) \"\n              f\"acc {acc_d:.2f} vs nokey {acc_0:.2f}\")\n\n    best_li = max(per_layer,\n                  key=lambda k: per_layer[k][\"_g\"][0][\"contrast_union\"].value)\n    sc, g_rand, acc_d, acc_0 = per_layer[best_li][\"_g\"]\n    g2_flip = bool(acc_d > acc_0)\n    rand_ok = bool(g_rand.verdict)\n    verdict = fn_index_verdict(gate0, sc, g2_flip, rand_ok)\n    for k in per_layer:\n        del per_layer[k][\"_g\"]\n    print(f\"[fni] best layer L{best_li} (selection-corrected \"\n          f\"alpha={alpha_sel}) -> VERDICT: {verdict}\")\n\n    result = {\n        \"model_id\": args.model_id, \"seed\": args.seed, \"scale\": S,\n        \"key_scale\": args.key_scale, \"ref_layer\": L,\n        \"inj_layers\": inj_layers, \"alpha\": args.alpha,\n        \"alpha_selection_corrected\": alpha_sel,\n        \"valid_geo\": valid_geo, \"valid_animal\": valid_an,\n        \"union_size\": len(union), \"dropped_collisions\": sorted(drop),\n        \"key_norms\": key_norms, \"n_cells\": len(cells),\n        \"gate0\": gate0, \"per_layer\": per_layer, \"best_layer\": int(best_li),\n        \"g2_flip\": g2_flip, \"vs_random_ok\": rand_ok, \"verdict\": verdict,\n        \"cells\": records}\n    out = Path(args.out)\n    out.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    (out / \"fn_index.json\").write_text(\n        json.dumps(_json_safe(result), indent=2, allow_nan=False))\n    print(f\"[fni] wrote {out}/fn_index.json\")\n    return 0\n\n\ndef main() -> int:\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=\"P-FN-INDEX cross-family dispatch\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--validate\", action=\"store_true\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--model-id\", default=\"Qwen/Qwen3-4B\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--device\", default=\"mps\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--dtype\", default=\"bfloat16\", choices=[\"float32\", \"bfloat16\"])\n    ap.add_argument(\"--ref-layer\", type=int, default=9)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--scale\", type=float, default=2.0)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--key-scale\", type=float, default=2.0)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--n-per-domain\", type=int, default=0,\n                    help=\"cap items per domain (0 = all valid; smoke uses 6)\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--alpha\", type=float, default=0.05)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--seed\", type=int, default=0)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--out\", default=\"results/fn-index/qwen3-4b\")\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n    if args.validate:\n        return run_validate(args.alpha)\n    return run_model(args)\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    raise SystemExit(main())\n"
}

### Thinking

Looking at the performance metrics for the indexed-dispatch approach at the 4B scale, the dispatch mechanism shows substantial improvements across all measurement layers, with effect sizes ranging from +4.2 to +9.7 and highly significant p-values. The key finding is that at the L22 diagonal level, the indexed approach achieves 70% accuracy compared to zero for the baseline, meaning the key mechanism fundamentally corrects the model's output direction on neutral prompts where it would otherwise fail completely.

The selection criterion chose L27 based on the highest contrast value at +9.696, even though peak accuracy actually occurs at L22 with 0.70. Either layer confirms the advisory is valid since both show the indexed-dispatch working reliably above the baseline's zero performance. This is a compelling result for the 4B model with the hand-built three-exemplar keys, so I'm committing the code and smoke tests, then launching the full 32B evaluation with the expanded cell set across both geometric and animal categories, which should take roughly 30-40 minutes to complete.

### Thinking

I've found a strong indexed-dispatch advisory with highly significant contrast across all depths (p=0.0001), where injected keys achieve 70% diagonal accuracy compared to 0% without them, and I'm now committing and launching the 32B verdict.

### Thinking

Looking at the P-FN-INDEX 32B results from the frozen table benchmark, Gate-0 passed validation across both banks with perfect scores, and at every depth level the statistical tests show highly significant differences between the dispatch methods with d_union outperforming by over 5 basis points. The selection-corrected alpha threshold was exceeded by orders of magnitude, and the G2 flip test shows a dramatic accuracy improvement of 0.46 versus 0.06 without the key parameter.

Examining the per-map heterogeneity, class-level dispatch achieves near-perfect accuracy at 0.94 while continent-level dispatch is weakest at 0.17, suggesting the dispatch strength is highly content-dependent. The 32B model shows lower overall accuracy than the 4B smoke test (0.46 versus 0.70), but the 32B achieves better relative depth performance at layers 48-64 compared to the smoke test's layer 22 baseline. The depth analysis reveals a U-shaped accuracy curve with peaks at early (layer 19) and late (layer 48) positions and a dip in the middle layers, indicating the dispatch mechanism activates at the boundaries rather than mid-network.

The core finding confirms that function selection is content-addressable and the index structure exists, which unlocks progression to the next phase. I'm preparing the results for the program-plates page along with memory and state block candidates, then committing autonomously for approval.
